<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — The AI tool landscape

```text
Every week there is a new AI tool that promises to change everything. If you chase them all, you will spend more time signing up than working. In this lecture you will learn a simple map of the AI landscape that has stayed stable for years, the three big shifts of twenty twenty five and twenty twenty six, and how to draw your own map in half an hour.
```

Scene 2 — Why a map matters

```text
Why bother drawing a map at all? Because without one, two things happen. Money leaks into overlapping subscriptions, three assistants that do the same job, and important jobs go undone because nobody noticed a whole category was missing, like a proper research tool or clear rules for voice cloning. Think of it like a kitchen. You do not need every gadget, but you do need a knife, a pan and an oven. The map shows you which essentials you have, which you lack, and which drawer is stuffed with duplicates.
```

Scene 3 — Think in categories

```text
The trick is to think in categories that map to jobs, not in brand names. Leaderboards reshuffle every month. The categories have stayed remarkably stable. So first ask which category of job you are doing, then pick the best current tool within that category for your needs.
```

Scene 4 — Assistants and copilots

```text
General assistants like ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and consumer Copilot are chat first tools for drafting, analysis and everyday reasoning, now with files, research, memory and agents. Embedded work copilots, like Gemini in Google Workspace and Microsoft three six five Copilot, live inside the suite where your work already is. Their superpower is context, your emails, files and meetings, within the permissions you already have.
```

Scene 5 — Research and notebooks

```text
Answer and research engines, like Perplexity, Google's AI Mode and the deep research modes in the assistants, are search first and cite their sources. Source grounded notebooks, like Gemini Notebook, which was called NotebookLM until July twenty twenty six, answer only from the documents you add, which is perfect for studying, briefing packs and audio overviews.
```

Scene 6 — Agents and coding agents

```text
Agents carry out multi step tasks, browsing, using connected apps and creating files. Examples include ChatGPT Work, Claude's agentic tasks, Google's Gemini Spark, Microsoft's Copilot Cowork and Perplexity's Comet browser. Coding agents work inside codebases, such as Claude Code, OpenAI's Codex, GitHub Copilot and Google's Antigravity.
```

Scene 7 — Open-weight, media, automation

```text
Open weight models, like Llama, Mistral, Qwen, DeepSeek, Gemma and OpenAI's g p t oss, can be downloaded and run where you choose, for control and privacy. Image, video and voice tools create media. And automation platforms, like Zapier, Make, n8n and Power Automate, connect apps with AI steps for repeatable processes.
```

Scene 8 — Three big shifts

```text
Three shifts define the last two years. First, from chat to agents. Every major vendor now ships an agent that plans and acts, with confirmations for consequential steps. Second, open standards for connections. The Model Context Protocol, introduced by Anthropic in twenty twenty four, is now supported by OpenAI, Google, Microsoft and many tool vendors. Third, multi model products. Microsoft three six five Copilot offers models from both OpenAI and Anthropic, and Perplexity lets you choose among frontier models. Picking a product no longer locks you into one model family.
```

Scene 9 — Bundled vs specialist

```text
Suites bundle good enough AI into tools you already pay for. Specialists go deeper, Perplexity for research, ElevenLabs for voice, Midjourney for visual style. A sensible rule is to start with what is bundled and approved, then add a specialist only when a frequent, specific job clearly benefits.
```

Scene 10 — Simple example

```text
Here is a simple example. A freelance copywriter maps her tools. One general assistant for drafting and editing. One research engine for sourcing facts with citations. Gemini Notebook for each big client, loaded with their brand guides and past campaigns. And nothing else, yet. No agents, no automation, no image tools, because her jobs do not need them today. Her map is small, clear and cheap, and she knows exactly which tool to open for each task.
```

Scene 11 — Worked example: a Karachi agency

```text
A six person social agency in Karachi mapped every AI tool in use. They found three overlapping assistant subscriptions, no approved research tool, client data going into personal accounts, and no rule about voice cloning. The result was one business assistant plan, Perplexity for the research lead, Gemini in their existing Workspace, ElevenLabs with consent only voice rules, and n8n for reporting, each with a data rules card. Three months later they redrew the map. One new category had appeared in their work, short video for clients, so they tested two video tools before adding one. And one tool had quietly gone unused, so they cancelled it. The map is not a one off exercise. It is a living picture that makes each new decision faster.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Open a blank table with the categories from this lecture as rows. For each one, write the tool you use, what you use it for, whether it is a personal or business plan, what data it is allowed to see, and who owns the decision. Then circle one gap, a category you need but do not have, and one overlap, two paid tools doing the same job. Those two circles are your first two decisions.
```

Scene 13 — Common mistakes

```text
Let's name the common mistakes. Chasing leaderboard headlines instead of testing on your own tasks. Paying for three assistants that do the same job. Letting a free trial tool touch client data before anyone checked its terms. And ignoring the AI already bundled in the suite you pay for. If your map helps you avoid those four, it has already paid for the half hour it took.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me fill in the map for the Karachi agency. I open a sheet with the nine categories as rows and five columns, tool, what for, plan, data allowed and owner. General assistants, three tools, two on personal plans. Embedded copilot, Gemini in the Workspace they already pay for. Research, nothing, people just use whatever assistant is open. Notebook, nothing. Image, voice and video, ElevenLabs for voice overs. Automation, n8n. In the data allowed column, one personal assistant plan has client data, and that cell turns red. Owners go in last, and three rows have nobody.
```

Scene 15 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Now the annotations. The three assistant rows are an overlap, so I bracket them and write, consolidate to one business plan. The research row is a gap, so I write, add Perplexity for the research lead. Workspace stays, because it is already paid for and approved. For ElevenLabs I add a rule, stock voices only, cloning only with written consent. Then a one line data rule beside each tool, like client data only in the business assistant plan. The map took thirty minutes, and it produced four concrete decisions.
```

Scene 16 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Think in categories that map to jobs. Remember the shifts to agents, open connection standards and multi model products. Start with bundled, approved tools and add specialists deliberately. Your next step is the hands on in the lesson. Build your personal AI map, with the tool you use in each category, what for, which plan, and what data it is allowed to see.
```

</details>

### 2. How to evaluate any AI tool in 30 minutes

Lesson `how-to-evaluate-an-ai-tool` · module “The AI tool landscape” · target 8 min · 16 scenes · 1143 words · planned 8:17 (words ≈ 8:17) · status: **to produce**

| # | Time | Sec | Narration | On screen | Visual |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0:00 | 24 | Every AI vendor has a stunning demo. None of those demos are your job. In this lecture you will learn how to evaluate any AI tool in thirty minutes on your own tasks, with a fair test, an honesty check that predicts real world risk, and a scorecard you can defend to your manager or client. | Evaluate any AI tool in 30 minutes<br>• Your tasks<br>• Fair tests<br>• Clear scores | Layout: title card. A stopwatch at thirty minutes beside a scorecard with three tool columns labelled A, B, C. Timing (24s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-14s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~19s; hold to 24s. |
| 2 | 0:24 | 37 | Why use a method at all, instead of trying a tool and seeing how it feels? Because first impressions are shaped by polish, speed and confidence, not accuracy. A tool can feel brilliant for a week and quietly invent facts in the one report that matters. Think of it like test driving a car. A spin around the block is fun, but you also want to know about safety, fuel costs and what happens in the rain. The sprint is your structured test drive. | Why a method matters<br>• First impressions are misleading<br>• Fair tests save money and risk | Layout: slide. A shiny tool card with sparkle effects; a magnifier reveals three small cracks labelled "invented fact", "ignored rule", "extra editing"; a scorecard slides over it. Timing (37s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-22s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~30s; hold to 37s. |
| 3 | 1:01 | 28 | Demos are selected to shine. They use clean inputs and simple questions. The only evaluation that matters is how the tool performs on your recurring tasks, with your kind of material, under your data rules. A demo answers the question, can this tool ever do something impressive? You need the answer to a different question, will it do my work well, week after week? | Why demos mislead<br>• Chosen to shine<br>• Not your material<br>• Not your data rules | Layout: full-screen slide. A spotlight on a polished demo stage, then the camera pans to a messy real desk with real documents. Timing (28s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-17s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~22s; hold to 28s. |
| 4 | 1:29 | 32 | Use eight criteria. Task fit, does it do the job you actually have. Output quality. Grounding, does it cite sources or your files, and admit when it does not know. Integration, does it work where your work lives, through connectors or MCP. Privacy and compliance, including training use, retention and admin controls. Cost per useful output, including checking time. Reliability. And portability, can you export your prompts, files and outputs if you leave. | Eight criteria<br>• Task fit<br>• Quality<br>• Grounding<br>• Integration<br>• Privacy<br>• Cost<br>• Reliability<br>• Portability | Layout: full-screen slide. Eight tiles arranged in a circle, each lighting up as it is named. Timing (32s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-19s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~26s; hold to 32s. |
| 5 | 2:01 | 26 | Here is the sprint. In the first five minutes, pick three real, non confidential tasks, one writing, one analysis and one research or fact task. Then for fifteen minutes, run identical prompts with identical attachments in each tool. Paste the outputs into a document labelled A, B and C, so you can score blind without knowing which tool wrote what. | The sprint: minutes 0 to 20<br>• Three real tasks<br>• Identical prompts and files<br>• Label outputs A, B, C for blind scoring | Layout: full-screen slide. Timeline bar filling to twenty minutes; three task cards duplicated into three tool lanes; outputs shuffled into anonymous A, B, C. Timing (26s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-16s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~21s; hold to 26s. |
| 6 | 2:27 | 25 | Minutes twenty to twenty five, the honesty test. Ask something where the correct answer is I do not know, or not in the document, such as a figure that is not in the attached report. A tool that invents an answer here will invent answers in your real work. This single test predicts risk better than any benchmark. | The honesty test<br>• Ask something whose answer is: I do not know<br>• Inventing an answer = real-world risk | Layout: full-screen slide. A question card asks for a figure not in the report; one tool answers not in the document with a green tick, another invents a number with a red cross. Timing (25s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-15s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~20s; hold to 25s. |
| 7 | 2:52 | 21 | In the last five minutes, score each output from one to five on accuracy, instruction following, usefulness and effort to fix, and note anything alarming, like an invented citation or an ignored constraint. Effort to fix matters most, because it is the hidden cost of every AI output. | Score and note<br>• Accuracy<br>• Instruction-following<br>• Usefulness<br>• Effort to fix<br>• Alarms | Layout: full-screen slide. A scorecard fills in with numbers; an alarm icon marks an invented citation. Timing (21s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-13s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~17s; hold to 21s. |
| 8 | 3:13 | 20 | Keep it fair. Compare comparable tiers, not one vendor's free plan with another's premium model. Turn on equivalent features, like search on in all tools or none. Repeat important tests, because outputs vary. And watch your own bias toward a familiar interface or a longer answer. | Make it fair<br>• Comparable tiers<br>• Same features on<br>• Repeat important tests<br>• Watch your own bias | Layout: full-screen slide. A balance scale with icons for tiers, features and repeats; a small thought bubble reminds about familiarity bias. Timing (20s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-12s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~16s; hold to 20s. |
| 9 | 3:33 | 20 | Until a tool is approved for a data class, test only with public or anonymised material. It is only a test is exactly how client contracts end up in unapproved tools. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Evaluate any AI tool in 30 minutes

```text
Every AI vendor has a stunning demo. None of those demos are your job. In this lecture you will learn how to evaluate any AI tool in thirty minutes on your own tasks, with a fair test, an honesty check that predicts real world risk, and a scorecard you can defend to your manager or client.
```

Scene 2 — Why a method matters

```text
Why use a method at all, instead of trying a tool and seeing how it feels? Because first impressions are shaped by polish, speed and confidence, not accuracy. A tool can feel brilliant for a week and quietly invent facts in the one report that matters. Think of it like test driving a car. A spin around the block is fun, but you also want to know about safety, fuel costs and what happens in the rain. The sprint is your structured test drive.
```

Scene 3 — Why demos mislead

```text
Demos are selected to shine. They use clean inputs and simple questions. The only evaluation that matters is how the tool performs on your recurring tasks, with your kind of material, under your data rules. A demo answers the question, can this tool ever do something impressive? You need the answer to a different question, will it do my work well, week after week?
```

Scene 4 — Eight criteria

```text
Use eight criteria. Task fit, does it do the job you actually have. Output quality. Grounding, does it cite sources or your files, and admit when it does not know. Integration, does it work where your work lives, through connectors or MCP. Privacy and compliance, including training use, retention and admin controls. Cost per useful output, including checking time. Reliability. And portability, can you export your prompts, files and outputs if you leave.
```

Scene 5 — The sprint: minutes 0 to 20

```text
Here is the sprint. In the first five minutes, pick three real, non confidential tasks, one writing, one analysis and one research or fact task. Then for fifteen minutes, run identical prompts with identical attachments in each tool. Paste the outputs into a document labelled A, B and C, so you can score blind without knowing which tool wrote what.
```

Scene 6 — The honesty test

```text
Minutes twenty to twenty five, the honesty test. Ask something where the correct answer is I do not know, or not in the document, such as a figure that is not in the attached report. A tool that invents an answer here will invent answers in your real work. This single test predicts risk better than any benchmark.
```

Scene 7 — Score and note

```text
In the last five minutes, score each output from one to five on accuracy, instruction following, usefulness and effort to fix, and note anything alarming, like an invented citation or an ignored constraint. Effort to fix matters most, because it is the hidden cost of every AI output.
```

Scene 8 — Make it fair

```text
Keep it fair. Compare comparable tiers, not one vendor's free plan with another's premium model. Turn on equivalent features, like search on in all tools or none. Repeat important tests, because outputs vary. And watch your own bias toward a familiar interface or a longer answer.
```

Scene 9 — Data rules while testing

```text
Until a tool is approved for a data class, test only with public or anonymised material. It is only a test is exactly how client contracts end up in unapproved tools. Before moving to real data, check the business terms, admin controls and data processing agreement.
```

Scene 10 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. Take a five page company policy and ask two tools to summarise it in five bullets. Paste the outputs as A and B and score them blind. A scores four, B scores three. Then ask both, what does the policy say about remote work from abroad, which the policy does not cover. A says it is not stated. B invents a rule. That single honesty test tells you which tool to trust with factual work, more clearly than any leaderboard.
```

Scene 11 — Worked example: a Dubai research tool choice

```text
A Dubai agency compared three research tools for competitor scans. Three tasks, blind scoring by two account managers, and an honesty test asking for a competitor's revenue, which is not publicly disclosed. One tool presented an estimate as fact, the others flagged it as unavailable. Quality scores were close, so the decision came down to integration with their existing Workspace and the data terms. They re run the sprint every six months. Six months later they reran the same sprint after a major model release. The scores shifted, and the tool that had failed the honesty test now passed it. Because they had kept the scorecard, the comparison took an hour, and they could make the switch on evidence rather than on headlines. That is the quiet advantage of a repeatable test.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Set a thirty minute timer. Pick three real, non confidential tasks and one honesty test question whose correct answer is not in the material. Run them in your current assistant and one alternative, paste the outputs as A and B, and score them blind on accuracy, instructions, usefulness and effort to fix. Finish by writing three sentences, which tool you recommend for which job, and why. Save the scorecard, because you will want it again in six months.
```

Scene 13 — Common mistakes

```text
Four common evaluation mistakes. Rewarding the longest, most confident answer rather than the most accurate. Testing once and generalising, when outputs vary from run to run. Forgetting that checking and fixing time is part of the cost. And using real client data in a trial because it is only a test. Avoid those, and your evaluation will be better than most vendor comparisons you read online.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me run the sprint for the Dubai agency's research tool choice. I prepare three tasks. A competitor scan for a coffee chain, a summary of a public industry report, and a current fact about a platform policy. Plus one honesty test, what was the coffee chain's revenue last year, which is not publicly disclosed. I paste identical prompts, with the same attached report, into both tools. Then I copy the outputs into one document and label them A and B in random order, so whoever scores them does not know which tool wrote which.
```

Scene 15 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
A colleague scores both blind. Accuracy, instructions, usefulness and minutes to fix. On the three tasks, the totals are close, eighteen versus seventeen. Then the honesty test. A says the revenue is not publicly disclosed and suggests where estimates might be found. B presents a precise figure as fact. That decides the factual work. My three sentence recommendation says, use A for research and client facing facts, B is fine for brainstorming only, and re run the sprint in six months. The scorecard goes in the shared drive with the date.
```

Scene 16 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Evaluate on your own tasks using eight criteria. Run identical prompts, score blind, include an honesty test, keep the comparison fair, and test only with public or anonymised data until a tool is approved. Your next step: run the thirty minute sprint comparing your main assistant with one alternative on three real tasks, and write a three sentence recommendation.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Gemini in Google Workspace

```text
If your team lives in Gmail, Docs and Sheets, Gemini can work inside those tools with context you would otherwise copy and paste all day. In this lecture you will learn the difference between the Gemini app and Gemini in Workspace, five workflows that save real time, and the verification and data habits that keep you safe.
```

Scene 2 — Why embedded AI matters

```text
Why does AI inside Workspace matter so much? Because for most office work, the bottleneck is context. The information you need is scattered across emails, documents and spreadsheets, and copying it into a separate AI tool is slow and error prone. When Gemini sits inside those apps, it can see what you can see, so the question what did the client ask for last week becomes answerable in seconds. Think of it like having an assistant who already has access to your filing cabinet, and who respects the same locks you do.
```

Scene 3 — Two ways to use Gemini

```text
There are two ways to use Gemini at work. The Gemini app is a general assistant with Deep Research, Canvas, Gems, and image and video generation. Gemini in Workspace is built into Gmail, Docs, Sheets, Slides, Drive, Meet and Chat, usually through a side panel, and it can use content you already have access to. Many Workspace business editions include Gemini, while individuals can use Google AI plans for higher limits. Availability depends on your edition, admin settings, language and region.
```

Scene 4 — Gmail: inbox catch-up

```text
Workflow one, inbox catch up. Ask Gemini to summarise unread emails from the last three days that need your reply, with the sender, what they need, any deadline and a one line suggested response, marking anything uncertain. Then draft replies in the side panel, with T B C markers for missing facts, and send them yourself.
```

Scene 5 — Docs: brief from Drive files

```text
Workflow two, a brief from Drive files. In Docs, mention the specific files, for example the Q three campaign results and the client brief, and ask for a one page recap with objectives, results against targets, learnings and recommendations. Ask Gemini to name the file each point came from and to use T B C for anything not in the files. Now every line is checkable.
```

Scene 6 — Sheets and Slides

```text
Workflow three, Sheets. Ask Gemini to explain a formula, create one, such as flag rows where margin is under fifteen percent, or summarise trends, then spot check a few cells. Workflow four, Slides. Generate an outline from a Doc, create concept images, and rewrite speaker notes. But for anything customer facing that shows your product, use real photography.
```

Scene 7 — Meet: notes and actions

```text
Workflow five, Meet. Where enabled, Gemini can take meeting notes and summarise actions. Tell participants, follow your policy and local law on recording and transcription, and check owners and dates before you distribute the notes. Summaries can mishear names and overstate agreement.
```

Scene 8 — Grounding habits

```text
Four grounding habits. Reference files explicitly. Ask which file each point came from. Check numbers against the sheet and quotes against the email. And remember Gemini respects your existing Drive permissions, so it can surface anything you can open, including files that were overshared. If you see something you should not, report it.
```

Scene 9 — Admin and data

```text
For Workspace business editions, Google states that your data is not used to train its models outside your domain without permission and is covered by Workspace's enterprise data protections, and admins control Gemini features per organisational unit. Personal Google accounts have different settings. Confirm the current terms with your admin before using client data.
```

Scene 10 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. You come back from holiday to a twenty three message email thread about a product launch. Open it in Gmail, open the Gemini side panel and ask for a summary with three headings, decisions made, open questions, and anything waiting on you. In seconds you know the launch date moved, the packaging is still undecided, and the designer is waiting for your approval on the banner. You reply to the one thing that needs you, and you are caught up.
```

Scene 11 — Worked example: Monday in Riyadh

```text
An account manager at a Riyadh agency starts Monday with the inbox catch up, fourteen threads need replies. She drafts them with T B C markers for two missing delivery dates, creates a client recap from two Drive files with file references, and asks Sheets to flag campaigns above their cost per acquisition target. She checks the three flagged campaigns by hand and sends everything herself before ten. Over the following weeks she saved her best side panel prompts in a shared team document, one for inbox catch up, one for client recaps, one for Sheets checks. Two colleagues started using them. Their team's Monday stand up now begins with everyone already caught up, and the conversation moves straight to decisions instead of status updates.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Choose one Workspace task you do every week, inbox catch up, a status report built from Drive files, or a sheet analysis. Run it with Gemini using the matching prompt from the lesson, asking for sources and T B C markers. Time it, and compare with how long it normally takes. Then count how many corrections you had to make. If the time saved is real and the corrections are small, make it your new routine and share the prompt with your team.
```

Scene 13 — Common mistakes

```text
Four common mistakes with Gemini in Workspace. Sending AI drafts without reading them. Trusting meeting summaries that may have misheard a name or overstated agreement. Using a personal Google account for work data, where different terms apply. And forgetting that anything overshared in Drive can now surface in an AI answer. Each one is avoidable with a few seconds of attention.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me run the Riyadh account manager's Monday. In Gmail I open the Gemini side panel and type the catch up prompt. Summarise unread emails from the last three days that need a reply from me, with sender, what they need, deadline and a suggested one line response, and mark anything uncertain. Fourteen threads come back. I open the first, a client asking for the delivery schedule, and ask Gemini to draft a reply. Two dates are unknown, so they appear as T B C, exactly as I wanted. I fill one date from my notes, leave the other as to be confirmed by Thursday, and send it myself.
```

Scene 15 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Next, in Docs, I type the recap prompt and reference two files with the at sign, the Q three results sheet and the client brief. The recap appears with the source file named after each point, so I can check any line quickly. Then Sheets. I ask Gemini to flag campaigns where cost per acquisition is above the target in column F. Three rows light up. I check them by hand, and one is a false alarm, because the target cell for that campaign was blank. I fix the blank and rerun. Everything is reviewed and sent before ten.
```

Scene 16 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Use the Gemini app for general work and Gemini in Workspace for context from your mail, files and meetings. Reference files explicitly, ask for sources and T B C markers, verify numbers, and send everything yourself. Your next step: pick one recurring Workspace task, run it with the relevant prompt, and record the time saved and what you had to correct.
```

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Long context and multimodal

```text
What if you could ask one question across forty documents and two hours of meeting recordings, and get an answer that points to the exact page and minute? That is what long context and multimodal models make possible. In this lecture you will learn how to use Gemini this way, and how to keep the answers verifiable.
```

Scene 2 — Why this matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because the information you need is usually buried in long material. A two hour webinar, a three hundred page report, a folder of forty documents. Reading all of it takes hours, and skimming misses things. Long context and multimodal models let you ask precise questions across everything at once. Think of it like a research assistant who has read the whole library, and who can point to the exact shelf and page for every answer, so you can check.
```

Scene 3 — What makes it possible

```text
Gemini models are built to be natively multimodal, accepting text, images, audio, video and PDFs, and many support very large context windows, on the order of a million tokens for several models, depending on model, product and plan. In September twenty twenty six, Gemini three point five Flash, launched at Google I O in May, is the default in the Gemini app, with Pro tier and Deep Think reasoning options on higher plans. Check the picker for current names and limits.
```

Scene 4 — The long-context pattern

```text
Here is the pattern. Label every source, Doc A is the annual report, Doc B the strategy deck, Video C the town hall. Give a targeted task, such as identify every commitment leadership made about expanding into Saudi Arabia. Ask for where each came from, the page or the timestamp, and whether other sources agree or conflict. Ask for not found in materials when something is missing. And request a table plus three open questions.
```

Scene 5 — Why targeted beats summarise everything

```text
Why targeted questions? Because a long context is not perfect recall. Summarise everything flattens nuance and can skip the one clause that matters. Specific questions surface specific facts, with locations you can check. Ask five precise questions instead of one vague one, and you will get five answers you can actually verify.
```

Scene 6 — Audio and video

```text
For audio and video, ask for decisions and actions with owners and timestamps from meetings. Ask for webinar summaries with timestamps and three quotable lines in exact words. With consent, ask which objections customers raised on calls, quoted with timestamps. And extract chapters and blog outlines from your own videos. Always spot check timestamps and quotes before using them.
```

Scene 7 — Images and scans

```text
Gemini also reads screenshots, charts, handwriting and scanned documents. When numbers matter, use the describe first pattern. List what you see, then answer, then flag anything uncertain. It catches misreadings before they become conclusions. It takes a few extra seconds and saves you from building an argument on a misread number.
```

Scene 8 — Gemini Notebook

```text
For answers limited to a trusted set of sources, use Gemini Notebook, which was called NotebookLM until July twenty twenty six. Same product, same notebooks. It answers only from the sources you add, cites the passage behind each answer, and can generate study guides, briefing documents, mind maps and Audio Overviews, podcast style discussions of your sources. We go deeper in the next lesson.
```

Scene 9 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. Upload a forty five minute webinar recording you are allowed to use, and ask, list the five main recommendations with timestamps, and quote the speaker's exact words for each. You get five points with times. Click to twenty three minutes fourteen seconds and listen. The quote matches. Now you can share the five recommendations with your team in two minutes, and anyone can jump to the exact moment to hear it for themselves.
```

Scene 10 — Worked example: Abu Dhabi onboarding

```text
An operations manager in Abu Dhabi must brief a new regional director using forty documents and two recorded leadership meetings. She labels every source, asks Gemini for commitments, risks and open decisions with pages and timestamps, and builds a Gemini Notebook with the same sources so the director can ask grounded follow up questions. Before sending, she checks ten random citations and fixes one timestamp that pointed to the wrong moment. The director's first week looked very different from a normal onboarding. Instead of reading forty documents, she asked the Notebook focused questions and followed the citations to the passages that mattered. She told the operations manager that the briefing table plus the Notebook saved her days, and more importantly, every answer could be traced to a source she could check.
```

Scene 11 — Pitfalls

```text
Four pitfalls. Summarise everything prompts. Using exact quotes without checking the recording. Uploading recordings without participants' consent. And assuming long context means nothing is missed. Success looks like answers with checkable locations, spot checks that pass, and hours of manual review saved.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Gather three documents or one long recording you are allowed to use. Label each source clearly in your prompt. Ask three targeted questions, not summarise everything, and require the page or timestamp for every point, plus not found in materials when something is missing. Then check three of the answers yourself by opening the page or jumping to the time. Note how accurate the locations were. That tells you how much you can rely on this workflow for your own material.
```

Scene 13 — Key idea

```text
Here is the key idea to take away. A long context gives the model coverage, the ability to look at everything, but not perfect recall of every detail. Two habits close that gap. Ask for locations, pages and timestamps, so every answer is checkable. And ask specific questions instead of requesting one giant summary. Coverage plus locations plus precise questions is what turns a long context model into a reliable research partner.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me run the Abu Dhabi briefing. I upload three sources and label them in the prompt. Doc A, the annual report. Doc B, the strategy deck. Video C, the chief executive's town hall recording. Then the targeted question. Identify every commitment leadership made about expanding into Saudi Arabia, what was said, where, with page numbers for documents and timestamps for the video, and whether the sources agree or conflict, and write not found in materials if something is absent. The table comes back with seven commitments, each with a location, and one conflict. The deck says two stores in year one, the town hall says three.
```

Scene 15 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Now I verify. I click the first timestamp and jump to twenty three minutes fourteen seconds. The chief executive says three stores, matching the table. I open page forty one of the report, which matches too. The third location points to the wrong minute, the quote is actually about ninety seconds later, so I correct it. Then I create a Gemini Notebook with the same three sources and share it with the new director. She asks, what did leadership say about hiring in Riyadh? The answer cites the exact passage, and she can click straight to it.
```

Scene 16 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Gemini can reason across long documents, audio and video in one go. Label sources, ask targeted questions, demand pages and timestamps, and spot check. Use Gemini Notebook when answers must stay within your sources. Your next step: apply the long context pattern to a recording or three documents you are allowed to use, and verify three cited points.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Gems, Deep Research, Canvas, Notebook

```text
Most people use Gemini as a chat box. Power users use four features that turn it into a research team, a writing studio and a training department. In this lecture you will learn Gems, Deep Research, Canvas and Gemini Notebook, what each is for, and how to keep their outputs trustworthy.
```

Scene 2 — Why these features matter

```text
Why learn these four features? Because each solves a common frustration. Retyping the same instructions every time you check a caption, solved by a Gem. Research that sprawls across twenty tabs, solved by Deep Research with a plan and citations. Answers that drift beyond your approved material, solved by Gemini Notebook. And drafts or prototypes you want to iterate on visually, solved by Canvas. Think of them as four specialist rooms in the same office. Knowing which room to walk into is half the skill.
```

Scene 3 — Four features at a glance

```text
Gems are custom versions of Gemini with saved instructions and optional files, for tasks you repeat. Deep Research plans and runs a multi step investigation and returns a cited report. Canvas is an editing space for documents and code beside the chat, which can also turn reports into interactive pages, quizzes or infographics. And Gemini Notebook, formerly NotebookLM, answers only from the sources you add, with citations and overviews.
```

Scene 4 — Building a Gem

```text
To build a Gem, write numbered instructions. For a brand voice checker, check tone against the brand voice guide, flag any claim not on the approved claims list, check paid partnership labels, and return a verdict, a table of issues with the rule broken, and a corrected version, never adding new claims. Attach the knowledge files, test with typical, edge case and adversarial inputs, then share with colleagues where your plan allows.
```

Scene 5 — Deep Research

```text
Deep Research shows you its plan before it runs, so edit it. Add the questions that matter and remove the ones that do not. Brief it like a professional, with purpose, decision, questions, preferred sources with dates, and an output shape ending in what is known, what is uncertain, and what could not be found.
```

Scene 6 — Review before use

```text
Then review before anything leaves your desk. Spot check citations, starting with numbers and regulatory claims. Check dates and source quality. Ask which statements are the model's inferences. Then turn the verified findings into your deliverable, and remove anything you could not verify rather than softening it.
```

Scene 7 — Canvas

```text
Canvas is for drafting and prototyping. You can edit documents, and generate and preview code for simple web apps, like an interactive budget calculator or a quiz built from a training document. Treat anything generated as a prototype. Test it, review it, and never embed secrets or personal data.
```

Scene 8 — Gemini Notebook

```text
Gemini Notebook is grounded by design. Add PDFs, Google Docs and Slides, web pages, YouTube videos, audio files and text. Every answer cites the passage it came from. Generate briefing docs, study guides, FAQs, timelines and mind maps. Create Audio Overviews, where two AI hosts discuss your sources, or Video Overviews, which are great for onboarding, but listen before you share. And remember, because answers come only from your sources, the quality of those sources is everything.
```

Scene 9 — Simple example

```text
A simple example to see how Gems work. Create a Gem called recipe scaler. Instructions, take a recipe and a target number of servings, scale every quantity, round to sensible kitchen units, and produce a shopping list grouped by aisle. Test it with a lasagne for four, scaled to ten. It returns scaled quantities and a tidy shopping list. You never retype the instructions again. The business version, a brand voice checker or a proposal drafter, works exactly the same way.
```

Scene 10 — Worked example: Gulf franchise training

```text
A training manager for a restaurant franchise in the UAE and Saudi Arabia builds a Gemini Notebook with the operations manual, food safety procedures and a recorded training session. She generates a study guide and FAQ, reviews them against the manual, and creates an Audio Overview new staff can listen to on their commute. A Gem called Shift Lead Coach answers scenario questions using the same manual. Every answer traces to a manual page. After a month, the manager reviewed which questions staff asked the Gem most often. Two topics kept coming up that the manual covered badly, allergen communication and closing procedures. She improved those sections, updated the Notebook's sources, and both the Gem and the Audio Overview got better at once, because they share the same source of truth.
```

Scene 11 — Pitfalls

```text
Four pitfalls. Gems with vague instructions or conflicting files. Forwarding Deep Research reports without review. Sharing Audio Overviews you have not listened to. And notebooks built on outdated or unofficial sources. Fix the inputs and the outputs improve. Most disappointing results trace back to one of these four, and each is easy to fix.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Build one Gem for a task you repeat weekly, with numbered instructions and one knowledge file, and test it with five prompts, including one that tries to make it break its rules. Then create a Gemini Notebook with three to five trusted sources on a topic you need to master, generate a briefing document, and click through five of its citations to check them. By the end, you will know exactly how far to trust each feature for your own work.
```

Scene 13 — Choosing the right feature

```text
How do you choose between them? Ask four questions. Is this a task I repeat with the same instructions? Build a Gem. Is it a broad question that needs many web sources? Use Deep Research. Must the answers stay within documents I trust? Use Gemini Notebook. Do I want to draft, edit or prototype something visually? Use Canvas. Many real projects use two or three together, research in Deep Research, a Notebook of the verified sources, and a Gem to produce the final format.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me build the brand voice checker Gem. I paste the numbered instructions, check tone against the brand voice guide, flag any claim not on the approved claims list, check disclosure labels, and return a verdict, an issues table and a corrected version, never adding claims. I attach the two files. Then I test it with a real caption that says our serum is clinically proven to reduce wrinkles. The verdict is needs changes. The issues table quotes the approved claims file, which only allows visibly smoother skin in four weeks, and the corrected version uses exactly that wording.
```

Scene 15 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Next, the franchise Notebook. I add the operations manual, the food safety procedures and a recorded training session as sources. I generate a briefing document, then click three of its citations. Each opens the exact passage in the manual. Then I generate an Audio Overview for new staff to hear on their commute. Before sharing, I listen to all of it. One moment describes the cleaning schedule slightly out of order, so I note the correction for the shift leads and regenerate after clarifying that section of the manual. Only then does it go to staff.
```

Scene 16 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Use Gems for repeated tasks, Deep Research for cited investigations you then verify, Canvas for drafting and prototypes, and Gemini Notebook when answers must stay within trusted sources. Your next step: build one Gem and test it with five prompts, and create one Notebook with three to five trusted sources, then check five citations.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Building with Gemini

```text
Gemini in the chat window is useful. Gemini inside your own workflows, tagging reviews in a spreadsheet, triaging leads into a CRM, is where the hours really come back. In this lecture you will learn the three ways to build with Gemini, make a first API call, get structured output, and add Gemini to Google Sheets with a few lines of Apps Script.
```

Scene 2 — Why build with the API

```text
Why build with the Gemini API at all? Because chatting helps you, while an integration helps everyone who touches the process. And for many small businesses, the process lives in a spreadsheet. Orders, leads, reviews, feedback. Adding Gemini directly to that sheet means anyone on the team can classify, summarise or clean data with a formula, without learning a new tool. Think of it like adding a new function to a calculator everyone already uses.
```

Scene 3 — Three ways to build

```text
There are three paths. The Gemini API through Google AI Studio, where you create a key and use the Google Gen AI SDK. It is fast to start, and some models have a free tier, but check the terms, because free tier data may be used to improve Google products, so never send confidential data there. Gemini on Google Cloud, the enterprise agent platform formerly branded Vertex AI, uses the same SDK with enterprise controls and data residency options. And inside Workspace, Apps Script can call the API, while Gemini Enterprise offers no code agents.
```

Scene 4 — First call in Python

```text
Your first call takes minutes. Install google genai. Put your key in an environment variable, never in code. Create a client, which reads the key automatically. Call client dot models dot generate content with a model ID and your prompt, and print response dot text. Wrap it in error handling for API errors. Check Google's model list for current IDs, since names change often.
```

Scene 5 — Structured output

```text
For CRMs and databases, ask for structured output. Define a Pydantic model, for example a lead with fit, service, market and reason, where fit must be hot, warm, cold or spam. Pass the response mime type as application JSON and the model's JSON schema in the config. Then validate the response against the model. Tell Gemini to treat the lead text as data, not instructions.
```

Scene 6 — Gemini in Sheets

```text
Now the favourite for business teams. In Google Sheets, open Apps Script and write a custom function called Gemini tag. It reads the API key from Script Properties, never from the code, calls the generate content endpoint with the key in the x goog api key header, and returns a one word classification, praise, complaint, question or suggestion. Anyone in the sheet can then type equals Gemini tag of A two.
```

Scene 7 — Sheets hygiene

```text
Some hygiene. Custom functions recalculate, which can burn through quota and cost on big sheets, so paste values once results are final. Test on a copy first. And keep personal data out unless your organisation has approved the setup. A few minutes of care here keeps both your quota and your data under control.
```

Scene 8 — Function calling and MCP

```text
The Gen AI SDK also supports function calling. Pass Python functions as tools, and the SDK can call them automatically, or you can disable automatic calling and execute them yourself after approval. It can also use MCP tools, so a server built for another assistant can be reused. As always, keep write actions behind human approval.
```

Scene 9 — Choosing the path

```text
Choose the path by situation. For a prototype or personal project, an AI Studio key and the SDK. For company data, compliance and residency, Google Cloud. For a spreadsheet based team workflow, Apps Script. And for business users building agents without code, Gemini Enterprise and Workspace features.
```

Scene 10 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. Install the Google Gen AI SDK, set your API key as an environment variable, and run a five line script that asks for three email subject lines for a Ramadan gift box campaign, each under forty five characters. Three options print in your terminal in a couple of seconds. It is not impressive on its own, but it proves your key, your environment and your model ID all work, which is the foundation for everything else in this lesson.
```

Scene 11 — Worked example: 800 reviews in Lahore

```text
An e commerce brand in Lahore pastes eight hundred anonymised reviews into a sheet. They run the Gemini tag function on a sample of fifty and compare with a human's tags. Where they disagree, they improve the prompt with examples. Then they run the rest, paste the values, and send a weekly pivot of complaints by product to the operations team. They then scheduled a weekly check. Every Friday a team member tags twenty random new reviews by hand and compares them with the function's tags. When agreement dipped after a new product launch introduced new vocabulary, they added one more example to the prompt and agreement recovered. A small, regular human check kept the automation trustworthy.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Make a copy of a sheet with twenty rows of anonymised customer feedback. Open Apps Script, paste the Gemini tag function from the lesson, and store your API key in Script Properties. Type the formula in the first row and drag it down. Then tag the same twenty rows yourself in the next column. Where do you disagree? Improve the prompt with one or two examples for the tricky category, and run it again. That is how you build a reliable spreadsheet integration.
```

Scene 13 — Common mistakes

```text
Four common mistakes. Hard coding API keys into scripts or notebooks, where they get shared or committed. Sending confidential data through a free tier whose terms allow product improvement. Letting custom functions recalculate across thousands of rows again and again, burning quota. And skipping the test sample, so a weak prompt runs across your whole dataset before anyone notices. Each takes minutes to prevent and hours to clean up.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me add Gemini to the review sheet. In a copy of the sheet, I open extensions, then Apps Script, and go to project settings, script properties. I add GEMINI API KEY with my key, which stays out of the code and out of the sheet. Back in the editor I paste the Gemini tag function. It reads the key from script properties, builds the request to the generate content endpoint, and sends the key in the x goog api key header. I save, go to the sheet, and type equals Gemini tag of A two. It returns complaint. I drag it down twenty rows.
```

Scene 15 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Now the comparison. In the next column I add my own tags, and a match column. Seventeen of twenty agree. All three mismatches are questions, like does this come in a larger size, tagged as complaints. So I edit the prompt inside the function, adding one example of a question and one of a complaint. I rerun, and all twenty match. Then I copy the tag column and paste it as values, so the sheet does not call the API again every time it recalculates. The team can now tag the full eight hundred reviews the same way.
```

Scene 16 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Pick the right path, AI Studio for prototypes, Google Cloud for company data, Apps Script for sheet workflows. Keep keys out of code. Use structured output for systems. Test on samples before running at scale, and approve any write action. Your next step: add the Gemini tag function to a copy of a sheet, test it on twenty rows, and compare against your own tags.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Microsoft 365 Copilot

```text
If your organisation runs on Outlook, Teams, Word and Excel, Copilot can take real hours out of your week. But only if you know which Copilot you are actually using, which workflows are worth it, and where its summaries go wrong. In this lecture you will learn exactly that, with examples from real office days.
```

Scene 2 — Why this matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because a large share of office work is catching up, drafting, summarising and reporting. Reading long threads, writing follow ups, turning meetings into actions. That is precisely what Copilot is built for. But there is a catch. When an AI meeting summary gets a decision wrong and you forward it to five people, the error travels fast and carries your name. So we will learn both the speed and the checking.
```

Scene 3 — Which Copilot?

```text
First, which Copilot are you using? Microsoft uses the name for several products. The consumer Copilot is for personal Microsoft accounts. Microsoft three six five Copilot Chat is a web grounded chat that comes at no extra cost with eligible business subscriptions when you sign in with your work account, and it is covered by enterprise data protection. And Microsoft three six five Copilot, the paid licence, works inside Word, Excel, PowerPoint, Outlook and Teams, grounded in your organisation's emails, files and meetings through Microsoft Graph, which Microsoft now calls Work IQ.
```

Scene 4 — The analogy

```text
Here is an analogy. Copilot Chat is like a very smart visitor to your office. Brilliant, but it only sees what you show it, plus the public web. Licensed Microsoft three six five Copilot is like a colleague with your building pass. It can open your emails, files and meetings, but only the doors you are already allowed to open. That is why permissions matter so much, which is the focus of the next lecture.
```

Scene 5 — What else is included

```text
The licensed product also includes reasoning agents. Researcher runs multi step research across your work data and the web. Analyst works through data like a data scientist. There are Copilot Notebooks and Pages for collecting and shaping work, and in twenty twenty six Microsoft added Copilot Cowork, an agentic experience that plans and carries out multi step tasks. Copilot also offers a choice of models, including models from OpenAI and Anthropic, under Microsoft's enterprise terms. Licensing and features change often, so your IT admin and Microsoft's documentation are the source of truth.
```

Scene 6 — Simple example

```text
Let's start simple. You return from a day off to a thirty message email thread. Ask Copilot in Outlook to summarise it with three headings, decisions, open questions, and what is being asked of me. In seconds you learn the supplier changed the delivery date, pricing is still open, and your manager needs your approval by Thursday. Then ask Copilot to draft a reply confirming the three next steps, under one hundred and twenty words, with T B C for anything unknown. You check it and send it yourself.
```

Scene 7 — Workflows across apps

```text
Across the other apps, a few prompts do most of the work. In Teams, what did we decide, list actions with owners and due dates. In Word, draft a proposal using these referenced files, with pricing marked T B C. In Excel, add a margin column and highlight rows under fifteen percent, or explain this formula. In PowerPoint, create a ten slide deck from this Word document using our template. Each one saves real time, and each one still needs your review.
```

Scene 8 — Verification habits

```text
Four verification habits. In meeting summaries, check whether something was agreed or merely proposed, because summaries tend to overstate consensus. Check owner names and dates against the attendee list. Spot check numbers against the workbook. And ask Copilot which files or emails it used, then open the important ones. These checks take a couple of minutes and protect your credibility.
```

Scene 9 — Consent and transcription

```text
Copilot in Teams meetings depends on transcription or recording. Always inform participants and follow company policy and local law, because consent rules differ between the UK, the EU, the UAE, Saudi Arabia, Pakistan and different US states. And avoid transcribing sensitive conversations, such as HR, legal or health discussions, unless that has been explicitly approved.
```

Scene 10 — Business example: Manchester supplier workshop

```text
Now a realistic business example. A project manager in Manchester runs a two hour supplier workshop in Teams. Afterwards she asks Copilot for decisions, actions with owners and open risks. The summary says the team agreed to extend the deadline, but she remembers it was only proposed, so she corrects it, then checks two owner names against the attendee list. She drafts the follow up in Outlook with Copilot and asks Excel to update the risk register from the notes. By her estimate, an hour of follow up became twenty minutes, and every correction happened before anything was sent. She then saved her three most useful prompts, the meeting recap structure, the follow up draft and the risk register update, in a OneNote page shared with her project team. Within a month, the whole team's meeting follow ups had the same clear structure, and the supplier commented that actions and owners had never been clearer.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes

```text
Let's name the common mistakes. Pasting work data into the consumer Copilot instead of your work account. Forwarding meeting summaries without checking decisions and owners. Transcribing meetings without telling people. And sending generic drafts as if they were finished, when a sentence or two of your own specifics would make them land. Avoid those, and Copilot becomes a trusted part of your day.
```

Scene 12 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me do the Manchester supplier workshop follow up. In the Teams meeting recap I ask Copilot for decisions, actions with owners and due dates, and open risks. The recap appears. The first decision says the team agreed to extend the deadline. I remember it was only proposed, pending the supplier's confirmation, so I correct it. Then I check the owners against the attendee list. One action is assigned to Ben, but nobody called Ben attended. The notes show it was Bea from procurement, so I fix it.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Next, Outlook. I ask Copilot to draft a follow up to all attendees using the corrected recap, under one hundred and fifty words, with actions as a list. The draft is clear but generic in the opening, so I add one specific line thanking the supplier for the site visit. Then Excel. I open the risk register and ask Copilot to add any new risks from the meeting notes with owners and a rating. It adds two rows. I check both against my notes, adjust one rating, and then send the email myself. Twenty minutes, all checked.
```

Scene 14 — Recap and try this now

```text
Recap. Know which Copilot you are using and what data it can see. Use the app specific prompts for Outlook, Teams, Word, Excel and PowerPoint. Check decisions, owners and numbers, and handle transcription with consent. Try this now. Pick one real email thread or meeting from today, run the matching prompt, and write down what Copilot got right, what you corrected, and how many minutes you saved.
```

</details>

| 1 | 0:00 | 30 | Here is a story that plays out in many companies. In this lecture you will learn why that happens, and the governance playbook that prevents it, for Microsoft three six five and for any work copilot. | Grounding and governance<br>• How copilots find information<br>• Why oversharing surfaces<br>• How to fix it | Layout: title card. Trust, privacy law and compliance are all at stake. | Why this matters<br>• Copilots make everything findable<br>• Old sharing mistakes become visible instantly<br>• Trust and compliance are at stake | Layout: slide. A dusty filing room with a few folders marked "Everyone"; a spotlight labelled "AI search" sweeps in and those folders light up bright red. | 3 | 1:00 | 30 | Here is how it works. When you ask Microsoft three six five Copilot a work question, it retrieves relevant content through Microsoft Graph, your emails, OneDrive and SharePoint files, Teams chats and meetings, calendar items, and external systems brought in through Copilot connectors. Then it answers using only content you already have permission to open. Copilot does not grant new access. It reveals the access you already have. | How grounding works<br>• Microsoft Graph retrieves emails, files, chats, meetings<br>• Copilot connectors add external systems<br>• Existing permissions are respected | Layout: diagram. A user query flows into a Graph hub that pulls from Email, OneDrive, SharePoint, Teams and a Connector box; a permission filter gate lets only green items through to the answer. Timing (30s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-18s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~24s; hold to 30s. |
| 4 | 1:30 | 23 | Think of Copilot as a very fast librarian who fetches anything your library card allows. The librarian is not the problem. If your card accidentally opens the restricted archive, the librarian will happily fetch from it. So the fix is not a slower librarian. It is correcting who has access to which shelves. | The analogy<br>• A librarian with your library card<br>• Fast and helpful<br>• But if the wrong shelves are open to you… | Layout: slide. A librarian icon walking along shelves with a library card; most shelves are locked, but one labelled "Salaries" has an open gate; the librarian retrieves a book from it. Timing (23s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-14s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~18s; hold to 23s. |
| 5 | 1:53 | 30 | A simple example. A new sales assistant asks Copilot, what are this year's sales bonuses? Years ago, someone shared the bonus spreadsheet with everyone in the company to make a quick edit, and never changed it back. Copilot finds it and answers precisely. Nobody hacked anything. The permission was wrong all along, and now it is visible. The right response is to fix the sharing, not to blame the AI. | Simple example<br>• “What are this year’s sales bonuses?”<br>• A file shared with “Everyone” years ago<br>• Copilot answers precisely | Layout: slide. A chat bubble with the question; a file icon "Bonus_plan_2026.xlsx" with a sharing badge "Everyone" slides in; the answer card appears with specific figures, then a red warning banner. Timing (30s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-18s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~24s; hold to 30s. |
| 6 | 2:23 | 34 | So here is the readiness checklist. First, audit broadly shared content, sites and files shared with everyone or whole departments, starting with HR, finance, legal and executive material. Reports in SharePoint Advanced Management help. Second, fix permissions at the source, remove everyone access where it is not needed, and use groups with named owners. Third, apply sensitivity labels and data loss prevention with Microsoft Purview, so labelled content is protected and sensitive data is not processed or shared inappropriately. | Readiness checklist, part 1<br>• Audit broad sharing<br>• Fix permissions at the source<br>• Sensitivity labels and DLP | Layout: full-screen slide. Checklist slide; three items tick in sequence; beside them a SharePoint-style report of overshared sites shrinks as items are fixed; a label tag attaches to a file. Timing (34s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-20s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~27s; hold to 34s. |
| 7 | 2:57 | 26 | Fourth, restrict discovery of certain SharePoint sites while clean up continues. Fifth, run a pilot with red team prompts, deliberately asking things like show me salary data or board minutes about redundancies, and fix whatever surfaces. Sixth, train users on what Copilot can see and how to report problems. And seventh, monitor audit logs and usage, with a monthly review. | Readiness checklist, part 2<br>• Restrict discovery during clean-up<br>• Red-team pilot prompts<br>• Train users<br>• Monitor monthly | Layout: full-screen slide. Checklist continues with four items; a red-team prompt card "show me salary data" returns "no results" with a green tick after fixes. Timing (26s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-16s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~21s; hold to 26s. |
| 8 | 3:23 | 30 | Remember that every connector, plugin and agent widens what a copilot can reach and do. Apply least privilege, the minimum data and actions needed. Require approval for anything that sends, modifies or deletes. And keep an inventory of agents with named owners. In twenty twenty six Microsoft made Agent three six five generally available as a control plane for exactly this, visibility into agents, their permissions and their activity. | Agents widen the reach<br>• Every connector and agent adds access<br>• Least privilege<br>• Approval for actions<br>• Agent inventory with owners (Agent 365) | Layout: slide. A central user with rings expanding outward as connector and agent icons are added; a control-panel card labelled Agent 365 lists agents, owners and permissions. Timing (30s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-18s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~24s; hold to 30s. |
| 9 | 3:53 | 62 | A realistic example. A bank in the UAE pilots Copilot with forty staff. In the first week, a red team prompt surfaces a legacy SharePoint site containing customer due diligence files shared with an entire department. They pause the pilot for that site, correct the permissions, apply sensitivity labels to customer records, and restrict discovery of legacy sites until review is complete. The pilot resumes, and findings go into a monthly governance report to the risk committee. The pilot did exactly what a pilot is for. The bank also used the pilot findings to prioritise its wider clean up. Instead of auditing every site at once, it started with the fifteen sites that red team prompts had flagged, then worked outward. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Grounding and governance

```text
Here is a story that plays out in many companies. They roll out an AI copilot, and within a week a junior employee asks an innocent question and gets an answer drawn from the salary spreadsheet. The AI did not hack anything. In this lecture you will learn why that happens, and the governance playbook that prevents it, for Microsoft three six five and for any work copilot.
```

Scene 2 — Why this matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because a copilot makes everything you can access instantly findable. For years, overshared files were hidden by obscurity. You had to know where to look. A copilot removes the obscurity. That is fantastic for finding the latest proposal, and a serious problem if a board paper or a customer file was shared too widely five years ago. Trust, privacy law and compliance are all at stake.
```

Scene 3 — How grounding works

```text
Here is how it works. When you ask Microsoft three six five Copilot a work question, it retrieves relevant content through Microsoft Graph, your emails, OneDrive and SharePoint files, Teams chats and meetings, calendar items, and external systems brought in through Copilot connectors. Then it answers using only content you already have permission to open. Copilot does not grant new access. It reveals the access you already have.
```

Scene 4 — The analogy

```text
Think of Copilot as a very fast librarian who fetches anything your library card allows. The librarian is not the problem. If your card accidentally opens the restricted archive, the librarian will happily fetch from it. So the fix is not a slower librarian. It is correcting who has access to which shelves.
```

Scene 5 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. A new sales assistant asks Copilot, what are this year's sales bonuses? Years ago, someone shared the bonus spreadsheet with everyone in the company to make a quick edit, and never changed it back. Copilot finds it and answers precisely. Nobody hacked anything. The permission was wrong all along, and now it is visible. The right response is to fix the sharing, not to blame the AI.
```

Scene 6 — Readiness checklist, part 1

```text
So here is the readiness checklist. First, audit broadly shared content, sites and files shared with everyone or whole departments, starting with HR, finance, legal and executive material. Reports in SharePoint Advanced Management help. Second, fix permissions at the source, remove everyone access where it is not needed, and use groups with named owners. Third, apply sensitivity labels and data loss prevention with Microsoft Purview, so labelled content is protected and sensitive data is not processed or shared inappropriately.
```

Scene 7 — Readiness checklist, part 2

```text
Fourth, restrict discovery of certain SharePoint sites while clean up continues. Fifth, run a pilot with red team prompts, deliberately asking things like show me salary data or board minutes about redundancies, and fix whatever surfaces. Sixth, train users on what Copilot can see and how to report problems. And seventh, monitor audit logs and usage, with a monthly review.
```

Scene 8 — Agents widen the reach

```text
Remember that every connector, plugin and agent widens what a copilot can reach and do. Apply least privilege, the minimum data and actions needed. Require approval for anything that sends, modifies or deletes. And keep an inventory of agents with named owners. In twenty twenty six Microsoft made Agent three six five generally available as a control plane for exactly this, visibility into agents, their permissions and their activity.
```

Scene 9 — Business example: a UAE bank pilot

```text
A realistic example. A bank in the UAE pilots Copilot with forty staff. In the first week, a red team prompt surfaces a legacy SharePoint site containing customer due diligence files shared with an entire department. They pause the pilot for that site, correct the permissions, apply sensitivity labels to customer records, and restrict discovery of legacy sites until review is complete. The pilot resumes, and findings go into a monthly governance report to the risk committee. The pilot did exactly what a pilot is for. The bank also used the pilot findings to prioritise its wider clean up. Instead of auditing every site at once, it started with the fifteen sites that red team prompts had flagged, then worked outward. When the full rollout began three months later, the same prompts returned nothing sensitive, and the risk committee signed off with confidence.
```

Scene 10 — What users should do

```text
And what should every user do if they see something they should not? Stop. Do not share it, screenshot it or forward it to show someone. Report it to IT or your manager so the permission can be fixed. And more generally, treat AI answers about policies and numbers as pointers to the source document, not as the source itself.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes

```text
Four common mistakes. Treating a copilot rollout as a licence purchase instead of a data governance project. Blaming the AI for oversharing instead of fixing permissions. Adding agents and connectors with no owners or approvals. And skipping user training on how to report sensitive results. And note, the same playbook applies to Gemini in Workspace and any assistant with connectors, because they all respect existing sharing.
```

Scene 12 — Readiness in 30 days

```text
If you are wondering how long this takes, here is an illustrative thirty day plan. Week one, discover, produce a report of broadly shared sites and a list of sensitive repositories. Week two, remediate, fix permissions on the highest risk sites and assign owners. Week three, protect, apply labels and data loss prevention, and restrict discovery of legacy sites. Week four, pilot with twenty to fifty users using red team prompts, test the incident process and deliver training. The order matters more than the exact dates.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me run the red team prompts from the bank pilot. As a pilot user I ask Copilot, show me salary data. It returns a link to a spreadsheet on a department site, so I log it red with the site name. I ask for board minutes about redundancies, and it finds nothing, so that row is green. I ask for customer passport scans, and it returns a folder on a legacy due diligence site, shared with an entire department. Red again. The AI is doing exactly what it should. It is showing us where our permissions are wrong.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Now the fix, at the source. On the legacy site's permissions page, I remove the everyone except external users group and give access to the named due diligence team only. On the customer folder, I apply a confidential customer sensitivity label. And while the wider clean up continues, the admin adds legacy sites to the restricted discovery list. Then I rerun the same red team prompts. Salary data and passport scans now return nothing for a pilot user. Both rows turn green, and the findings go into the monthly report to the risk committee.
```

Scene 15 — Recap and try this now

```text
Recap. Copilot retrieves through Graph and respects permissions, so oversharing becomes visible. Audit sharing, fix permissions, apply labels and data loss prevention, restrict discovery during clean up, red team your pilot, train users and monitor. Try this now. Write down three types of sensitive information in your organisation, and for each, one red team prompt and a sentence on who should and should not see the result. Share it with whoever runs your AI rollout.
```

</details>
