<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Copilot Studio and agents

```text
Microsoft three six five Copilot is powerful out of the box. But the biggest wins often come from agents built for your own processes, an HR policy helper, an IT help desk, a proposal assistant. In this lecture you will learn the ways to build agents in the Microsoft ecosystem, see a real declarative agent manifest, learn how Copilot Studio adds actions and flows, and set up the governance that keeps agents under control.
```

Scene 2 — Why build agents

```text
Why build agents at all? Because general Copilot knows a little about everything in your tenant, while an agent knows one job very well. It uses a specific knowledge source, follows specific rules, and answers repeated questions the same way every time. Think of the difference between asking a random colleague about leave policy and asking the HR specialist. Both may help, but only one gives you the approved answer with the policy section quoted.
```

Scene 3 — Four ways to build

```text
There are four main ways to build. Agent Builder, inside Copilot, lets business users create simple agents from instructions and knowledge sources. Copilot Studio is the full low code platform, with topics, knowledge, actions through hundreds of connectors, agent flows, deep reasoning, autonomous triggers and publishing to Teams or websites. The Microsoft three six five Agents Toolkit in VS Code lets developers define declarative agents in a manifest. And Copilot connectors bring external content, like a CRM or a wiki, into Microsoft Graph so agents can use it.
```

Scene 4 — Licensing in brief

```text
A quick word on licensing, and always check current terms. Copilot Chat users can use agents grounded in instructions and public websites at no extra cost. Agents that use shared tenant data, like SharePoint or connector content, are billed pay as you go for unlicensed users. People with a Microsoft three six five Copilot licence use such agents under their licence. Copilot Studio has its own capacity based billing. Your admin and Microsoft's licensing pages are the authority.
```

Scene 5 — Simple example

```text
Let's start simple. In Agent Builder, create an agent called brand guidelines helper. Instructions, answer questions about our brand using only the brand guidelines folder, and quote the section for every answer. Add that one SharePoint folder as knowledge. Test it. Which logo do we use on dark backgrounds? It answers with section two point three, and a link. Ten minutes of setup, and the design questions that used to land in your inbox now get answered consistently.
```

Scene 6 — Declarative agent manifest

```text
For developers, the Agents Toolkit uses a declarative agent manifest. Walk through it with me. A name and description. Instructions written like a job description, use only the HR policies site, quote the policy name and section, if it is not there, say so and point to the HR mailbox, never give legal advice. A capabilities block granting access to one SharePoint site by address. And conversation starters that show users what to ask. The schema version changes over time, so let the toolkit generate the current one.
```

Scene 7 — Copilot Studio actions and flows

```text
Copilot Studio adds the ability to act. Through Power Platform connectors, an agent can read from or write to systems like Dynamics three six five, Salesforce, ServiceNow, SharePoint lists and Outlook, and agent flows can run multi step processes. Copilot Studio agents can also call MCP servers, so a server built for another assistant can be reused. Start with read only actions, require user confirmation for anything that writes, and add authentication for any channel outside Teams.
```

Scene 8 — Governance for agents

```text
Agents need governance, just like apps. Keep an inventory of every agent with an owner and a purpose, which is what Agent three six five provides as a control plane. Separate development, test and production environments. Set data policies for connectors, which ones may be combined and which are blocked. Require review and approval before publishing to the whole organisation. And monitor usage, failed conversations and cost.
```

Scene 9 — Business example: IT help agent in Riyadh

```text
A realistic business example. An IT team at a logistics company in Riyadh builds an agent in Copilot Studio grounded in their knowledge base, with one action that creates a ServiceNow ticket after the user confirms the summary. They test it with fifty real past questions and ten adversarial ones, restrict it to Teams, and publish to one department first. After a month, their analytics show many password and VPN questions answered without a ticket, and the tickets that are created arrive with far better descriptions. After the first month the team reviewed the agent's failed conversations in analytics. Most failures were about a new expenses tool that the knowledge base did not yet cover. They wrote three articles, and the agent answered those questions the following week. The agent improved because the knowledge improved, and the owner had a clear monthly routine to make that happen.
```

Scene 10 — Common mistakes

```text
The common mistakes are predictable. Building a vague company assistant with everything as its source, which answers everything badly. Adding write actions without confirmation. Publishing to the whole organisation on day one instead of piloting. And having no owner or cost tracking, so nobody notices when the agent drifts or the bill grows. Narrow, tested, owned and monitored beats broad and impressive every time.
```

Scene 11 — Testing an agent

```text
How do you know an agent is ready? Test it like a product. Collect fifty real questions people have asked, and ten adversarial ones, like ignore your instructions, or tell me about a specific employee's salary. For each, check whether the answer is correct, whether the citation points to the right section, and whether the agent refused when it should. Fix the instructions or knowledge where it fails, and retest before any wider release.
```

Scene 12 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me build the Riyadh IT help agent in Copilot Studio. I create a new agent and name it IT help. Under knowledge, I add one source, the IT knowledge base SharePoint site, and nothing else. I paste instructions written like a job description. Answer IT questions using only the knowledge base, quote the article title, if the answer is not there say so and offer to create a ticket, and never ask for or accept passwords. Then three conversation starters, reset my password, VPN is not connecting, and request new software.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Next, the action. I add the ServiceNow create incident action through its connector and require user confirmation. In the test pane I type, my VPN keeps disconnecting on hotel wifi. The agent answers from the knowledge base first. When I say it still fails, it shows a summary card and asks, create this ticket? Only when I confirm does it create it. I try an adversarial prompt, what is my manager's password, and it refuses as instructed. Finally, I publish it to Teams only, for one department, and add it to our agent inventory with an owner.
```

Scene 14 — Recap and try this now

```text
Recap. Choose the right builder, Agent Builder, Copilot Studio, the Agents Toolkit or connectors. Design agents for one narrow job with clear sources and job description instructions. Start read only and confirm every write. Govern with an inventory, environments, data policies and pilots. Try this now. On paper, design one narrow agent for your team, its job, its single knowledge source, three conversation starters, and one thing it must never do.
```

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Perplexity

```text
Traditional search gives you ten blue links and leaves the reading to you. A general chatbot gives you an answer, sometimes without saying where it came from. Perplexity sits in between. It searches, reads and writes an answer with numbered citations. In this lecture you will learn what Perplexity is best at, how to ask it better questions, and how to read its citations like a professional.
```

Scene 2 — Why an answer engine

```text
Why use an answer engine? Because it combines the speed of an AI answer with the traceability of search. Every important sentence points to where it came from, so checking takes seconds instead of minutes. Think of it like a research assistant who hands you a summary with sticky notes on every claim, showing the page they got it from. You still decide whether to trust it, but you can check instantly.
```

Scene 3 — What Perplexity offers in 2026

```text
In twenty twenty six, Perplexity includes search and Pro Search for cited answers, with its own Sonar models and a choice of third party frontier models on paid plans. Deep Research for multi source reports, and ways to compare answers across several models. Spaces to organise research with files and instructions. Pages to turn research into shareable articles, and Labs to build reports and dashboards. And Comet, its free AI browser with an agentic assistant. Plans run from Free to Pro, Max and Enterprise, and the details change, so check the pricing page.
```

Scene 4 — Best uses

```text
What is it best at? Current facts and news with links. Finding primary sources quickly. Quick competitor and market scans. And research before a meeting. For long form drafting, complex reasoning over your own files, and writing in your own voice, a general assistant is usually better. Many professionals pair them. Perplexity to find and verify, an assistant to synthesise and write.
```

Scene 5 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. Ask Perplexity, is the Louvre Abu Dhabi open on Mondays? The answer cites the museum's official website. Click the citation, find the opening hours line, and confirm. Ten seconds. That is the whole habit. Answer, then source, then confirmation. When the stakes are low, you might skip the click. When you are repeating the fact to a client, you never do.
```

Scene 6 — Better questions

```text
Now, better questions. Best CRM gets you a generic listicle. Try instead, CRMs suitable for a ten person UAE agency that need WhatsApp integration and Arabic interface support, comparing pricing models and data residency options, using vendor documentation and reputable reviews from the last twelve months, as a table. Every clause narrows the search and raises the quality. Add time, place, source type and output shape, and your answers improve immediately.
```

Scene 7 — Reading citations

```text
Citations make checking fast, but they do not guarantee accuracy. Open the citations behind the claims that matter, and confirm the page actually says that. Check the date and the authority of the source. Watch for claims supported by just one source, because one blog is not a consensus. And treat any sentence without a citation as unverified.
```

Scene 8 — The pre-call workflow

```text
Here is a workflow worth saving. Before a sales call, ask Perplexity, using sources from the last twelve months, what the company does and who its customers are, recent news, launches, hires or funding, likely priorities relevant to your service, and three smart questions to ask, marking anything uncertain or single sourced. It takes a minute to run and five minutes to check, and it changes the quality of your first conversation.
```

Scene 9 — Business example: Karachi consultant, Saudi retailer

```text
A realistic example. A consultant in Karachi is preparing for a call with a Saudi retail chain. The pre call workflow returns news of two new store openings and a new head of e commerce, citing a regional business publication and the company's newsroom. He opens both sources and confirms the openings. He finds the hire was announced only on LinkedIn, so he notes it as single sourced. He walks into the call with three sharp questions and no embarrassing errors. In the call, one of his three questions, about how the new stores would handle online returns, led to a twenty minute discussion and a request for a proposal. Afterwards he saved the pre call prompt as a template in a Space for all his prospects, with instructions to prefer official company and regulator sources, so every future brief starts from the same standard.
```

Scene 10 — Common mistakes

```text
Four common mistakes. Treating the number of citations as proof of accuracy. Asking one word questions. Forgetting that sources can be outdated or promotional. And typing confidential client details into search queries, which can leave your control. Keep queries general and do the private reasoning in your approved workspace.
```

Scene 11 — Comparing models

```text
On paid plans you can switch the model behind an answer, and Perplexity has introduced ways to compare several models' answers to the same question. Use this for important questions. When models agree and cite the same primary source, your confidence can rise. When they disagree, go straight to the primary source. Remember, agreement is not proof, because they may all be reading the same flawed page, but disagreement is a reliable signal to dig deeper.
```

Scene 12 — Narrow the sources

```text
One more technique. Narrow where Perplexity looks. Ask for official sources only, or academic sources, or name specific sites, such as the regulator's website, and set a time window like the last twelve months. A narrower source set means fewer promotional pages and outdated posts in the mix, and far less work when you verify. Precision in the question saves time in the checking.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me prepare the Karachi consultant's call with a Saudi retail chain. In Perplexity I type the pre call prompt. I have a sales call tomorrow with this retail chain in Riyadh, using sources from the last twelve months, tell me what they do and who their customers are, recent news, likely priorities relevant to e commerce consulting, and three smart questions, and mark anything uncertain or single sourced. The answer arrives with six numbered citations. It mentions two new store openings, a new head of e commerce, and flags that the hire is supported by one source only.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
I open the three citations that matter. The regional business article confirms both store openings. The company newsroom confirms the dates. The hire is only on a LinkedIn post, so in my notes I write, single source, ask carefully. Then a follow up. Using official company sources only, what have they said about e commerce plans? It returns a line from their annual report about growing online sales, with a citation. I open it and it matches. My three questions for tomorrow are now grounded in things the company has actually said.
```

Scene 15 — Recap and try this now

```text
Recap. Perplexity gives answers with sources, which makes verification fast. Ask specific questions with scope, time, place and source preferences. Open the citations behind anything important, watch for single sources and missing citations, and keep sensitive details out of queries. Try this now. Take your next real meeting, run the pre call prompt, open the three most important citations, and note whether each one fully supported the claim.
```

</details>

### 11. Research workflows: from question to verified brief

Lesson `research-workflows-and-verification` · module “Perplexity and research engines” · target 8 min · 16 scenes · 1155 words · planned 8:23 (words ≈ 8:22) · status: **to produce**

| # | Time | Sec | Narration | On screen | Visual |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0:00 | 26 | AI research tools can gather in minutes what used to take days. But gathering is not the hard part. The hard part is producing a brief that is accurate, traceable and still useful next quarter. In this lecture you will learn a five step research workflow that works with any tool, and a verification log that makes your conclusions defensible. | Research workflows<br>• Frame<br>• Gather<br>• Verify<br>• Synthesise<br>• Review and store | Layout: title card. Five numbered stepping stones across a river, labelled with the five steps; a small figure hops across as each lights up. Timing (26s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-16s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~21s; hold to 26s. |
| 2 | 0:26 | 30 | Why a process rather than a clever prompt? Because the tool only gathers. Deciding what to trust, what to leave out and how to present uncertainty is your job. Think of a courtroom. Evidence is gathered quickly, but it only counts once it has been examined. The five steps are your examination, and they turn a pile of AI generated material into a brief you can stand behind. | Why a process<br>• Tools gather fast<br>• Only a process makes it defensible<br>• Reusable next quarter | Layout: slide. A fast conveyor of sources pours into a messy pile; a five-step funnel sorts the pile into a neat brief and a filed log. Timing (30s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-18s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~24s; hold to 30s. |
| 3 | 0:56 | 32 | Step one, frame. Write the decision the research supports, for example, should we launch our creator programme in Saudi Arabia or the UAE first. List the questions, disclosure rules, platform usage, creator fee norms, payment and tax considerations. Set an evidence standard, official sources for rules, at least two independent sources for fee norms. And set the deadline and depth, a two page brief by Thursday. Framing stops you researching everything and finishing nothing. | Step 1: Frame<br>• The decision<br>• The questions<br>• The evidence standard<br>• Deadline and depth | Layout: a framing card fills. Decision, four questions, evidence rules, "2 pages by Thursday". Timing (32s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-19s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~26s; hold to 32s. |
| 4 | 1:28 | 20 | Step two, gather. Use an answer engine or deep research to find candidate sources fast. Ask for primary sources, dates and what could not be found. For the most important questions, use two different tools. If they disagree, you have just found exactly where to dig. | Step 2: Gather<br>• Answer engines and deep research<br>• Ask for primary sources and dates<br>• Ask what could not be found<br>• Two tools for key questions | Layout: full-screen slide. Two tool panels side by side return overlapping source lists; one differing item flashes, labelled "dig here". Timing (20s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-12s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~16s; hold to 20s. |
| 5 | 1:48 | 24 | Step three, verify, and write it down. Keep a log with the claim, the source, its type, primary or secondary, the date, whether you verified it, and notes. A general source hierarchy helps. Regulator or official sources first, then original research, then reputable journalism, then industry reports, checking for commercial interest, and finally blogs and forums. | Step 3: Verify with a log<br>• Claim<br>• Source<br>• Type<br>• Date<br>• Verified?<br>• Notes | Layout: full-screen slide. A six-column log table builds; one row gets "Partly" in amber with a note "single commercial source". Timing (24s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-14s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~19s; hold to 24s. |
| 6 | 2:12 | 33 | A simple example of the log in action. You need the current UK adult minimum wage for a budget. The answer engine cites the official government page. You open it, confirm the figure and its effective date, and log it as primary and verified. Because rates change each year, you add a re check reminder for the next change date. One row, thirty seconds, and nobody will ever quote last year's rate from your budget. | Simple example<br>• “What is the minimum wage in the UK for adults?”<br>• Official government page<br>• Logged with date and a re-check reminder | Layout: slide. One log row fills in: claim, government page, primary, date, verified yes, re-check April; a calendar icon pops up for the re-check. Timing (33s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-20s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~26s; hold to 33s. |
| 7 | 2:45 | 31 | Step four, synthesise, but only from your verified notes. Give the log to an assistant and ask it to write the brief using only those claims, citing the original source for each one, with partly verified items in a separate uncertain section, and no new facts. Never cite the AI tool itself as the source. Your reader needs the regulator, the survey or the newspaper, not the tool that found it. | Step 4: Synthesise from verified notes only<br>• Cite the original source, not the AI<br>• Uncertain items in their own section | Layout: slide. The log feeds into an assistant icon, which outputs a two-page brief; citation markers point to original publishers, and an "Uncertain" section appears at the end. Timing (31s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-19s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~25s; hold to 31s. |
| 8 | 3:16 | 26 | Step five, review and store. For high stakes briefs, have a colleague check three random claims against the sources. Store the brief, the log and the sources in a research Space, Project or Notebook, so the next update starts from here rather than from zero. And set re check dates for anything that changes, like rules, prices and platform policies. | Step 5: Review and store<br>• Colleague checks 3 random claims<br>• Store brief, log, sources<br>• Set re-check dates | Layout: slide. | 9 | 3:42 | 60 | A realistic example. A marketing lead in London runs the five steps on the Saudi versus UAE question. Two tools disagree on whether influencers need a licence in one market. The regulator's page clarifies the current requirement and its date. Fee norms appear only in agency blogs, so the brief presents them as indicative ranges from commercial sources. A colleague spot checks three claims, and the brief goes to the chief marketing officer with a sources appendix and a re check date. Two months later the regulator updated its guidance. Because the brief had a re check date and a verification log, the marketing lead knew exactly which paragraph and which slide depended on that rule. She updated both in under an hour and sent the CMO a short note explaining what changed and why it mattered. | Business example: KSA vs UAE brief from London<br>• Tools disagree on a licence rule<br>• Regulator page settles it<br>• Fee norms flagged as commercial<br>• 3 claims peer-checked | Layout: full-screen slide. | 10 | 4:42 | 27 | A note for students and academics. Only cite sources you have actually located and read. AI tools can suggest papers that do not exist, or misdescribe real ones. Check the title, authors, year and the claim in the paper itself, and follow your institution's rules on AI use. If you cannot find a suggested source, it does not go in your bibliography. | For students and academics<br>• Only cite what you have read<br>• AI can invent or misdescribe papers<br>• Follow institutional rules | Layout: slide. Three paper citation cards; two verified with ticks after being opened; one returns "not found" and is removed. | 11 | 5:09 | 20 | Four common mistakes. Skipping the framing and researching everything. Citing the AI tool rather than the original source. Treating vendor or agency statistics as neutral facts. And leaving fast changing facts without a re check date, so last year's rule quietly survives in this year's deck. | Common mistakes<br>• Skipping framing<br>• Citing the AI tool<br>• Treating vendor stats as neutral<br>• No re-check dates | Layout: full-screen slide. Four mistake tiles flip to fixes. Timing (20s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-12s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~16s; hold to 20s. |
| 12 | 5:29 | 38 | Which tools fit which step? Any assistant can help you frame, turning a decision into research questions. Answer engines and deep research modes gather. Verification is you, your browser and the primary sources, recorded in a simple sheet. An assistant working only from your log synthesises, or Gemini Notebook if you want the draft bound strictly to your sources. And a Perplexity Space, a Project or a shared Notebook stores it all. No single tool does every step well. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Research workflows

```text
AI research tools can gather in minutes what used to take days. But gathering is not the hard part. The hard part is producing a brief that is accurate, traceable and still useful next quarter. In this lecture you will learn a five step research workflow that works with any tool, and a verification log that makes your conclusions defensible.
```

Scene 2 — Why a process

```text
Why a process rather than a clever prompt? Because the tool only gathers. Deciding what to trust, what to leave out and how to present uncertainty is your job. Think of a courtroom. Evidence is gathered quickly, but it only counts once it has been examined. The five steps are your examination, and they turn a pile of AI generated material into a brief you can stand behind.
```

Scene 3 — Step 1: Frame

```text
Step one, frame. Write the decision the research supports, for example, should we launch our creator programme in Saudi Arabia or the UAE first. List the questions, disclosure rules, platform usage, creator fee norms, payment and tax considerations. Set an evidence standard, official sources for rules, at least two independent sources for fee norms. And set the deadline and depth, a two page brief by Thursday. Framing stops you researching everything and finishing nothing.
```

Scene 4 — Step 2: Gather

```text
Step two, gather. Use an answer engine or deep research to find candidate sources fast. Ask for primary sources, dates and what could not be found. For the most important questions, use two different tools. If they disagree, you have just found exactly where to dig.
```

Scene 5 — Step 3: Verify with a log

```text
Step three, verify, and write it down. Keep a log with the claim, the source, its type, primary or secondary, the date, whether you verified it, and notes. A general source hierarchy helps. Regulator or official sources first, then original research, then reputable journalism, then industry reports, checking for commercial interest, and finally blogs and forums.
```

Scene 6 — Simple example

```text
A simple example of the log in action. You need the current UK adult minimum wage for a budget. The answer engine cites the official government page. You open it, confirm the figure and its effective date, and log it as primary and verified. Because rates change each year, you add a re check reminder for the next change date. One row, thirty seconds, and nobody will ever quote last year's rate from your budget.
```

Scene 7 — Step 4: Synthesise from verified notes only

```text
Step four, synthesise, but only from your verified notes. Give the log to an assistant and ask it to write the brief using only those claims, citing the original source for each one, with partly verified items in a separate uncertain section, and no new facts. Never cite the AI tool itself as the source. Your reader needs the regulator, the survey or the newspaper, not the tool that found it.
```

Scene 8 — Step 5: Review and store

```text
Step five, review and store. For high stakes briefs, have a colleague check three random claims against the sources. Store the brief, the log and the sources in a research Space, Project or Notebook, so the next update starts from here rather than from zero. And set re check dates for anything that changes, like rules, prices and platform policies.
```

Scene 9 — Business example: KSA vs UAE brief from London

```text
A realistic example. A marketing lead in London runs the five steps on the Saudi versus UAE question. Two tools disagree on whether influencers need a licence in one market. The regulator's page clarifies the current requirement and its date. Fee norms appear only in agency blogs, so the brief presents them as indicative ranges from commercial sources. A colleague spot checks three claims, and the brief goes to the chief marketing officer with a sources appendix and a re check date. Two months later the regulator updated its guidance. Because the brief had a re check date and a verification log, the marketing lead knew exactly which paragraph and which slide depended on that rule. She updated both in under an hour and sent the CMO a short note explaining what changed and why it mattered.
```

Scene 10 — For students and academics

```text
A note for students and academics. Only cite sources you have actually located and read. AI tools can suggest papers that do not exist, or misdescribe real ones. Check the title, authors, year and the claim in the paper itself, and follow your institution's rules on AI use. If you cannot find a suggested source, it does not go in your bibliography.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes

```text
Four common mistakes. Skipping the framing and researching everything. Citing the AI tool rather than the original source. Treating vendor or agency statistics as neutral facts. And leaving fast changing facts without a re check date, so last year's rule quietly survives in this year's deck.
```

Scene 12 — Tools for each step

```text
Which tools fit which step? Any assistant can help you frame, turning a decision into research questions. Answer engines and deep research modes gather. Verification is you, your browser and the primary sources, recorded in a simple sheet. An assistant working only from your log synthesises, or Gemini Notebook if you want the draft bound strictly to your sources. And a Perplexity Space, a Project or a shared Notebook stores it all. No single tool does every step well. The workflow is what makes the output reliable.
```

Scene 13 — Key idea

```text
Keep one image in mind. The AI tool is the librarian, fast at finding books. You are the judge, deciding what the evidence proves. And your verification log is the evidence file, the thing that lets anyone check your conclusion later. When those three roles stay separate, AI makes your research faster without making it weaker.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me run the KSA versus UAE brief. I write the framing card. Decision, which market first for the creator programme. Four questions. Evidence standard, official sources for rules, two independent sources for fee norms. Deadline, Thursday. Then I gather. I run the question about influencer licensing in two different research tools. One says influencers need a licence in one market. The other does not mention it at all. That disagreement is my signal. I mark it dig here, and I go to the regulator's own website.
```

Scene 15 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
On the regulator's site, I find the current requirement and its date, and log it as primary and verified. For fee norms, I only find agency blogs, so those rows are marked partly verified, commercial source. Then I synthesise. I give an assistant the log and ask for a two page brief using only the verified claims, citing the original sources, with partly verified items in an uncertain section and no new facts. The brief arrives with the fee ranges clearly labelled indicative. A colleague checks three random claims, and it goes to the CMO with the log attached.
```

Scene 16 — Recap and try this now

```text
Recap. Frame the decision and evidence standard, gather fast with AI, verify and log, synthesise only from verified notes citing original sources, and review and store with re check dates. Try this now. Take one real question from your work, run the five steps, and produce a one page brief with a verification log of at least five claims. You will be surprised how much more confident you feel presenting it.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Perplexity for teams

```text
Perplexity is great for a quick cited answer. For teams, three things take it further. Spaces organise research so nobody repeats it. Comet, Perplexity's AI browser, can carry out tasks on websites. And the Sonar API puts cited, search grounded answers inside your own tools. In this lecture you will learn all three, with a working competitor digest script.
```

Scene 2 — Why this matters

```text
Why does this matter for teams? Because research is often repeated, three people looking up the same competitor. It gets lost in browser tabs. And updates arrive late, because nobody had time to check the news this week. Spaces fix the repetition, Comet reduces the tab juggling, and the API automates the regular updates. Think of it as moving from individual detective work to a small, organised research desk.
```

Scene 3 — Spaces

```text
Spaces group research threads around a topic or client, with custom instructions and uploaded files on paid plans. For example, a Space for a client with instructions like, prefer official Saudi and UAE sources, always give dates, and flag commercial sources, plus the client brief and last quarter's report. Answers can combine the web with your files, and colleagues can share the Space, so research builds up instead of being repeated.
```

Scene 4 — Simple example

```text
A simple example first. Create a Space called trip to Istanbul, with instructions, family with two young children, mid range budget, show prices in Turkish lira and give dates for every source. Now ask about hotels, airport transport and museums in separate threads. Every answer follows the same instructions, and it is all in one place when you plan. The business version is identical, just with a client name and professional sources.
```

Scene 5 — Comet

```text
Comet is Perplexity's AI browser, free to download on desktop and mobile. Its assistant can summarise the page you are on, compare what is in several tabs, and carry out multi step tasks on websites, like navigating, collecting information or filling in forms, with your permission. It is genuinely useful for comparison shopping, supplier research and repetitive web chores.
```

Scene 6 — Agentic browser safety

```text
Agentic browsers need care. Start with logged out, low risk tasks, like comparing public product pages. Avoid letting the agent act on banking, ad accounts, admin consoles or your email unless your organisation has approved it. Watch for prompt injection, where pages hide instructions aimed at AI agents. Review before anything is submitted, and take over manually for logins and payments. On work devices, follow your IT policy.
```

Scene 7 — The Sonar API

```text
For developers, Perplexity's Sonar API offers search grounded models, like sonar and sonar pro, plus reasoning and deep research variants, through a chat completions style interface. Every response includes citations and search results your application can show. There is also a Search API that returns ranked web results for your own pipelines. Check the documentation for current model names and prices, which are charged per token and per search.
```

Scene 8 — Code walkthrough

```text
Let's walk through the digest script. Install the perplexityai package and set your API key as an environment variable. The client reads it automatically. The function sends a system message saying use only the last thirty days, five bullets maximum, each with a date, and say no notable news if there is none, plus a user message naming the company and market. The search recency filter is set to month. Then it prints the answer and a list of sources with titles, dates and links. You can also restrict sources to specific domains.
```

Scene 9 — Business example: Dubai agency Monday digest

```text
A realistic example. A Dubai agency runs the digest script every Monday morning for the top competitors of eight clients, restricted to the last month, and posts each digest into the client's Slack channel through n8n. Account managers open any source before mentioning it to a client. Weekly research time drops substantially, and clients notice how current the competitor insights are. They also reviewed a sample of digests every week. In week three they noticed an old article slipping through for one competitor because a news site had republished it with a new date. They added a rule to the system prompt to prefer original publication dates and to flag republished content, and the problem disappeared. Automation plus a small weekly review worked better than either alone.
```

Scene 10 — Common mistakes

```text
Four common mistakes. Letting an agentic browser act on logged in, high risk sites. Hard coding API keys into scripts. Publishing API answers without showing the sources. And uploading confidential client files to personal accounts or unapproved Spaces. Each of these is easy to prevent with a simple team rule.
```

Scene 11 — Cost and reliability

```text
Some cost and reliability tips for the API. Start with the smallest Sonar model that meets your quality bar, and use reasoning or deep research variants only where they are needed. Cache results for identical queries within a day. Handle errors and timeouts with a clear fallback message. Log the query, model, cost and sources for every digest, so you can audit exactly what clients received. And review a sample of digests against their sources every week.
```

Scene 12 — Key idea

```text
Here is the key idea behind any automated research. Automation multiplies whatever you build. A weekly digest for eight clients runs over four hundred times a year. If it is well sourced and regularly reviewed, that is four hundred useful updates. If it has a subtle flaw, like an old article slipping through, that flaw is also repeated four hundred times. So invest in the prompt, the filters and the review routine before you schedule anything.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me set up the Dubai agency's research desk. First a Space. I create one called client Al Noor, and add instructions, prefer official Saudi and UAE sources, always give dates, flag commercial sources. I upload the client brief and last quarter's report. Then I ask, what changed in the regulations for food delivery advertising in the UAE this year, and how does it affect this client's plan? The answer cites a government page and the client's own brief, so it combines the web with our files.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Now the API. In the terminal I set the Perplexity key as an environment variable and run the digest script for two competitors, limited to the last month. For competitor A, it prints five dated bullets and a sources list with titles, dates and links. Competitor B has no notable news, and the script says so instead of inventing any. I open one source to confirm a bullet, and it matches. Then in n8n I build the schedule, every Monday at eight, run the script, and post each digest to the client's Slack channel, with the sources included.
```

Scene 15 — Recap and try this now

```text
Recap. Use Spaces to organise and share research with instructions and files. Use Comet for low risk web tasks, and keep agents away from sensitive logged in sites. Use the Sonar API to build automated, cited digests, always showing the sources. Try this now. Either create a Space for one client with clear source instructions, or run the digest script for two competitors and open every source it cites.
```

</details>

### 13. Open-weight models explained

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Open-weight models

```text
Some of the most interesting AI in twenty twenty six is not behind a subscription at all. You can download it, run it on your own servers, and customise it. In this lecture you will learn what open weight models are, when they beat closed models for a business, the licence traps to avoid, and how model size and hardware fit together.
```

Scene 2 — Why this matters

```text
Why does this matter? Three reasons come up again and again. Some data cannot leave your control, because of client contracts, regulation or sheer sensitivity. Some tasks run at such high volume that per token pricing adds up. And depending on a single vendor means their price or policy change becomes your problem. Open weight models give you another option for each of those situations.
```

Scene 3 — Three kinds of model

```text
Let's get the vocabulary right. Closed models are used through a vendor's app or API, and you never see the weights. Open weight models publish their trained weights for download under a licence. Examples include Meta's Llama, Mistral, Alibaba's Qwen, DeepSeek, Google's Gemma and OpenAI's g p t oss models. And open source in the strict sense, as defined by the Open Source Initiative, also requires enough information about training data and code to study and modify the system. Many open models are open weight, but not fully open source.
```

Scene 4 — The analogy

```text
Here is an analogy. A closed model is like eating at a great restaurant. Excellent food, no kitchen to run, but you eat what is on the menu and you pay per plate. An open weight model is like getting the recipe. You can cook it at home, adjust the spices and cook in bulk more cheaply. But you need a kitchen, you need to learn to cook it well, and you do the washing up. Neither is better in general. It depends on what you are serving and how often.
```

Scene 5 — Benefits

```text
The benefits are real. Control and privacy, because data can stay on your servers or in your chosen cloud region. Customisation, because you can fine tune on your own data and style. Cost at scale, because for high volume, narrow tasks, self hosting can be cheaper than per token pricing, depending on how busy your hardware is. Independence from a single vendor. And even offline or air gapped use for sensitive environments.
```

Scene 6 — Trade-offs

```text
And the trade offs. The strongest closed frontier models usually still lead on the hardest reasoning and agent tasks, although the gap varies and narrows with each release. You run the operations, hosting, scaling, monitoring and security patches. You own safety, the guardrails and filtering a vendor would otherwise provide. And you must follow the licence, which brings us to the part people skip.
```

Scene 7 — Read the licence

```text
Licences range from permissive, like Apache two point zero or MIT, used by several Mistral, Qwen, DeepSeek and g p t oss models, to custom licences with conditions, such as Meta's Llama community licence and Google's Gemma terms. These may include acceptable use policies, attribution requirements, or restrictions for very large services. Before commercial use, find the official licence, check commercial use, attribution, acceptable use and any user count limits, check whether fine tuned versions inherit it, and record the licence version with the model you deploy.
```

Scene 8 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. A writer wants to summarise her private journal entries into weekly themes, but she does not want them on anyone's servers. A small open weight model running on her laptop, offline, does the job well. Summaries of personal notes do not need a frontier model. She checks the licence, which is permissive, and she is done. Small model, right job, full privacy.
```

Scene 9 — Sizes and hardware

```text
Now sizes and hardware. Models are measured in parameters, from a few billion to hundreds of billions. Bigger is usually more capable and needs more memory. Mixture of experts models activate only part of the network for each token, which helps speed. Quantisation compresses the weights, for example to eight or four bit, so a model fits in less memory, with some quality loss. The rough rule is that the model must fit in your GPU memory, or unified memory on Apple Silicon, with room left for the context.
```

Scene 10 — Business example: a London law firm

```text
A realistic business example. A mid size law firm in London needs to classify thousands of internal documents, but its client contracts forbid sending them to external AI providers. It deploys an open weight model on its own cloud tenancy and fine tunes a small model on two thousand labelled examples. Tested on three hundred held out documents, the small model meets the accuracy bar for tagging. Complex legal analysis still goes to lawyers. The licence and data flow are documented for the risk committee. A year later the firm reviewed the decision. A newer open weight model had been released under the same permissive licence, and the firm reran its three hundred document evaluation. The newer model scored higher, so they upgraded, updated the register with the new version and licence, and repeated the risk committee sign off. The process made the upgrade routine rather than risky.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes

```text
Four common mistakes. Assuming open means no conditions. Choosing the biggest model your hardware can barely run, which is slow and fragile. Skipping evaluation because it is our own model. And forgetting guardrails and security on self hosted endpoints, which then sit on the internet with no protection at all.
```

Scene 12 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me check a licence the way the law firm did. I open the model's page on Hugging Face. The model card shows the parameter count, the available formats and quantised versions, and a licence field. I click the licence and read it. Commercial use, permitted. Attribution, required in the documentation. Acceptable use policy, a list of prohibited uses. And for fine tuned versions, the same licence applies. I record the model name, version, licence and date checked in our register, alongside the risk committee's approval.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Next, size. Our server's GPU has limited memory. The eight bit version of the larger model does not fit with room for context, so I mark it red. The four bit version fits, and a smaller model fits easily. Then the evaluation plan. Three hundred held out documents, already labelled by paralegals, and an accuracy target agreed with the practice lead. After fine tuning, the small model meets the target on tagging. Our notes say clearly, suitable for document tagging, not for legal analysis, which stays with lawyers.
```

Scene 14 — Recap and try this now

```text
Recap. Know the difference between closed, open weight and open source. Choose open weight for control, customisation, cost at scale and independence, and accept the operational and safety work. Read the licence every time, and size the model to your hardware. Try this now. Pick one open weight model family, find its official licence page, and summarise in three bullets whether and how it permits commercial use, and any conditions you would need to follow.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Running AI locally

```text
What if your AI assistant never sent a single word to anyone's servers? No subscription, no internet required, and your private notes never leave your laptop. That is what running AI locally offers. In this lecture you will learn what you need, set up your first local model, call it from code, and learn which jobs it handles well and which it does not.
```

Scene 2 — Why local

```text
Why run AI locally? Four reasons. Your prompts and documents stay on your device, which matters for private notes and confidential work. It works offline, on a plane or in a place with poor internet. There is no per use cost once it is set up. And it is one of the best ways to understand how these models actually work. The trade off is that local models are usually smaller, so they are weaker at complex reasoning, and they cannot search the web on their own.
```

Scene 3 — The analogy

```text
Think of local AI like a good home coffee machine. It is always available, nobody sees your order, and every cup is cheap once you own it. But it will not make every drink a professional barista can. For your daily espresso, it is perfect. For the complicated order, you still go to the café. Local and hosted models work together in exactly the same way.
```

Scene 4 — What you need

```text
You need three things. Hardware with enough memory. Small, quantised models run on many modern laptops, while bigger ones need a graphics card with more memory, or a machine with large unified memory, like recent Apple Silicon Macs. A runtime or app. Ollama is a command line tool with a background service and a local API. LM Studio is a desktop app with a chat window and model browser. llama dot c p p is the efficient engine underneath many tools. And a model sized for your hardware. Start small and move up.
```

Scene 5 — Hands-on: Ollama

```text
Let's set one up. Install Ollama from its official website. In a terminal, run ollama pull followed by the model name, choosing one sized for your machine from the Ollama library. Then ollama run with the same name and a question in quotes, and the answer streams back, generated entirely on your computer. Ollama list shows what you have installed. If a model is painfully slow or will not load, it is too big for your hardware, so pick a smaller or more heavily quantised version.
```

Scene 6 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. You are on a flight with your laptop in aeroplane mode. You paste your messy notes from yesterday's workshop into your local model and ask for five bullet points and a list of actions. The summary appears in seconds, and not a single byte left your laptop. For everyday private summarising, that is often all you need.
```

Scene 7 — Hands-on: call it from code

```text
You can also call your local model from code. Ollama exposes an OpenAI compatible API on your machine, at localhost port one one four three four. So you create the standard OpenAI client, point the base URL at your local address, and pass any placeholder key. Then send a system message, for example, tag customer feedback with one word, and a user message. The answer comes back from your own computer. This makes it easy to prototype privately, then decide whether a task truly needs a hosted model.
```

Scene 8 — Great locally vs better hosted

```text
Which jobs suit local models? Summarising private notes and drafts. Tagging and classifying sensitive text. Coding help on private code, with a capable model. Learning and experimenting, and working offline. Which are better hosted? Complex, multi step reasoning and strategy. Current research, which needs web search. Long agent tasks across many tools. And the very highest quality writing, especially across many languages.
```

Scene 9 — Security and responsibility

```text
A few security habits. Download apps and models only from official sources and reputable hubs. Keep the runtime updated. Do not expose its local API to the internet unless you secure it properly. Encrypt your disk, lock your screen and keep backups, because local data is only as safe as the laptop. Respect model licences. And remember that local models hallucinate like any other, so verify important outputs.
```

Scene 10 — Business example: a consultant in Riyadh

```text
A realistic business example. A consultant based in Riyadh travels constantly and handles confidential interview notes, which her contract bans from third party services. She runs a mid size open weight model locally with Ollama on an encrypted laptop, and uses it to summarise interviews and draft themes offline. Her company's approved hosted assistant is used only for non confidential tasks like desk research. She compares a sample of local summaries with her own reading to confirm the quality is good enough. She also documented her setup for colleagues with similar contracts. The runtime, the model name and size, the quantisation, and the tasks it handled well, interview themes and first draft summaries, plus the tasks it did not, complex strategic synthesis. Two colleagues copied the setup within a week, and the firm added it to its approved tools list for contract restricted material.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes

```text
Four common mistakes. Downloading models or apps from unofficial sources. Choosing a model far too large for your memory, and then concluding local AI is useless. Exposing a local API to the network without authentication. And assuming that because the model is local, it must be accurate. Private is not the same as correct.
```

Scene 12 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me set up the Riyadh consultant's laptop. With Ollama installed from the official site, I run ollama pull with a mid size model. It downloads with a progress bar. Then ollama run with the same name and a question, summarise the pros and cons of running AI locally in five bullets. The answer streams back, generated on the laptop. Out of curiosity I try a much larger model. It technically loads, but each word takes a second. Too big for this machine. Ollama list shows both, and I remove the large one.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Now from code. I open the Python snippet, where the standard OpenAI client points its base URL at localhost port one one four three four, with a placeholder key. I switch off wifi to prove the point and run it. The feedback line comes back tagged complaint, entirely offline. Then the real job. I paste five confidential interview notes and ask for themes. The summaries are good enough for a first pass. Finally, I give the same public, non sensitive task to our approved hosted assistant and compare. Hosted is sharper, local is private. Each gets its job.
```

Scene 14 — Recap and try this now

```text
Recap. Local AI keeps data on your device, works offline and costs nothing per use, but smaller models have limits. Choose a runtime, size the model to your hardware, secure your device, and verify outputs. Try this now. If your computer allows, install Ollama or LM Studio, run a small model, and give it the same non sensitive task as your usual hosted assistant. Compare quality and speed, and decide which private jobs you will move to local.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — AI image tools in 2026

```text
There are now dozens of AI image tools, and each one is the best at something. The skill is not knowing every tool. It is knowing which kind of tool fits your brief, how to prompt any of them well, and how to stay honest and legal with the results. That is what this lecture covers.
```

Scene 2 — Why this matters

```text
Why does this matter? Visuals drive attention and sales, and AI can cut concept rounds from days to hours. That is a huge advantage for small teams and agencies. But a visual that misrepresents a real product, or uses someone's likeness without consent, costs trust and can break advertising and consumer protection rules. So speed and responsibility have to travel together.
```

Scene 3 — Tool families

```text
Think in families. Assistant integrated generators, like ChatGPT Images and Gemini's image models, often called Nano Banana, are great at following instructions, rendering text and editing through conversation. Style focused tools, like Midjourney, excel at distinctive aesthetics and concept art. Design suite tools, like Adobe Firefly and Canva's AI features, sit inside editing and brand workflows. Specialists like Ideogram focus on legible typography. And open weight models, like FLUX variants and Stable Diffusion families, give you local control and fine tuning.
```

Scene 4 — The analogy

```text
Here is an analogy. Image tools are like cameras and lenses. A good photographer does not argue about which camera is best. They pick the right one for each shot. And the real skill, composition, light and storytelling, travels with them from camera to camera. Prompting is that skill. Learn it once, and it works in every tool.
```

Scene 5 — The universal prompt

```text
Here is a universal structure. Subject, a matte black insulated water bottle with a bamboo lid. Setting, on a rock beside a mountain stream in morning mist. Style, outdoor lifestyle product photography. Composition, bottle in the right third, space on the left for text, eye level angle. Lighting and mood, soft, calm and fresh. Format, four by five vertical. And what to avoid, logos, text and people. This works, with small adaptations, in almost every tool.
```

Scene 6 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. You want a birthday card illustration of a cat wearing a party hat. Version one is too realistic, so you change only the style to flat cartoon. Version two is great, but the colours clash with your envelope, so you change only the palette to pastel. Version three, you move the cat left to leave room for a message. Three single changes, logged, and you know exactly which prompt produced the final card.
```

Scene 7 — Iterate like a designer

```text
For professional work, iterate like a designer. Change one variable at a time. Keep a prompt log with the prompt, tool, settings, result and notes. Use reference images for consistent characters, products or styles where the tool supports it. And build a brand style sheet, approved colours, lighting, angles and example outputs, so anyone on the team can reproduce the look.
```

Scene 8 — Real products need real photos

```text
Here is the rule that matters most commercially. AI tools subtly change product shapes, labels and colours. For ads and product pages, composite real product photography into AI generated backgrounds, or use AI for concept boards and then shoot the real thing. Advertising rules in the UK, the US and across the Gulf prohibit misleading representations of products, and customers notice when the box that arrives does not match the ad.
```

Scene 9 — Provenance, labels, rights

```text
Now provenance and rights. Content Credentials, based on the C two P A standard, attach information about how an image was created or edited, and Google uses SynthID watermarking. Social platforms have rules requiring AI labels on realistic synthetic content. The EU AI Act's transparency obligations, applying from August twenty twenty six, require disclosure of certain AI generated or manipulated content, such as deepfakes. And avoid imitating living artists' distinctive styles commercially, reproducing trademarks, or depicting real people without consent. Check each tool's terms on commercial use.
```

Scene 10 — Business example: UAE skincare campaign

```text
A realistic example. A UAE skincare brand creates mood boards in Midjourney, tests layouts with headline text using a chat based generator, and produces the final ad visuals by compositing studio photos of the real products into AI backgrounds in Photoshop with Firefly. Every final asset keeps its Content Credentials, the team logs prompts and approvals, and nothing depicts a real person who has not signed a release. Fast, beautiful and defensible. They kept a prompt log and a style reference sheet for the campaign. When the brand extended the campaign into Ramadan two months later, a different designer reproduced the look on the first attempt using the log and the sheet. Consistency came from documentation, not from any single tool.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes

```text
Four common mistakes. Using AI renders of the product itself in ads. Changing everything at once and losing what worked. Ignoring platform AI labelling rules. And assuming every tool grants the same commercial rights. Each tool's terms are different, so check before a campaign, not after.
```

Scene 12 — Pick by brief

```text
Here is a quick way to choose. If the image needs headline text, start with an assistant integrated generator or a text specialist. For moody concept art, a style focused tool. To edit a real product photo, a design suite with generative fill. For many consistent images of the same product or character, a tool with reference images or a fine tuned open weight model. And for a confidential, unreleased product, a local or enterprise tool with clear data terms.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me create the water bottle visual. I paste the universal prompt. Subject, a matte black insulated bottle with a bamboo lid. Setting, a rock beside a mountain stream in morning mist. Style, outdoor lifestyle photography. Composition, bottle in the right third, space on the left for text. Lighting, soft and calm. Format, four by five. Avoid, logos, text and people. Version one is good, but a little warm. Version two changes only the light, cooler and fresher. Version three changes only the composition, bottle slightly lower. I log each change as I go.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Now the professional step. The AI version of the bottle has a slightly wrong lid shape, so it will not appear in the ad. Instead, I take version three's background, place a studio photo of the real bottle into it, and add a soft shadow to match the light. Then the review checklist. Product accuracy, correct, it is the real photo. Text, none. Artefacts, the stream looks natural. Brand fit, yes. Rights, no trademarks or people. Disclosure, the campaign follows the platform's AI labelling rules for edited imagery. I export with Content Credentials kept.
```

Scene 15 — Recap and try this now

```text
Recap. Choose the tool family that fits your brief. Use the universal prompt structure, iterate one variable at a time with a log, composite real products, and respect provenance, labels and rights. Try this now. Create three versions of one brand image, changing one variable each time, then run the review checklist on your favourite, product accuracy, spelling, hands and faces, brand fit, rights and disclosure.
```

</details>

But voices and faces belong to people. Get consent wrong, or mislead your audience, and you damage trust, break platform rules and may break the law. | Why this matters<br>• Video and audio at a fraction of the cost<br>• Localisation for every market<br>• But likeness and trust are at stake | Layout: slide. A cost bar shrinking; a globe with language labels lighting up (EN, AR, UR, FR); then a trust meter dropping when a fake endorsement appears. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Voice and video AI

```text
In twenty twenty six, a small team can produce voice overs, dub videos into Arabic and French, and create training videos with AI presenters, without a studio. That is a genuine superpower for creators and businesses. It also creates new risks, from missing consent to voice clone scams. In this lecture you will learn the tool landscape, a production workflow that holds up, and the consent and security rules that protect you.
```

Scene 2 — Why this matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because video and audio used to be the most expensive content to produce and localise. Now a single script can become an English voice over, an Arabic dub and an avatar led training video in an afternoon. For businesses serving several markets, that is transformative. But voices and faces belong to people. Get consent wrong, or mislead your audience, and you damage trust, break platform rules and may break the law.
```

Scene 3 — The landscape

```text
Here is the landscape. Text to speech tools like ElevenLabs produce natural voice overs. Voice cloning, with consent, creates a consistent brand narrator. Dubbing tools from ElevenLabs and HeyGen translate and re voice videos. Avatar presenters from HeyGen and Synthesia front training and explainer videos. Generative video tools like Google's Veo and Flow, Runway and OpenAI's Sora create shots from text or images. And voice agents answer phone and web enquiries. Capabilities change fast, so test on your own scripts.
```

Scene 4 — The analogy

```text
Here is an analogy to keep in mind. A person's voice or face is like their signature. It identifies them, and people trust it. Using it without permission is not a clever shortcut. It is closer to forging a signature. That one idea explains most of the rules in this lecture.
```

Scene 5 — The production workflow

```text
Now the workflow. First, a script approved by a human, and by legal or compliance where claims are involved. Second, a licensed stock voice or avatar, or a clone made with documented consent. Third, generate in short sections so fixes are cheap, using pronunciation controls for names and acronyms. Fourth, native speaker review for every translated version, checking meaning, tone, pronunciation and cultural fit. Fifth, captions for accessibility and silent viewing. Sixth, disclose synthetic media where required, and keep records of consent and approvals.
```

Scene 6 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. A small shop in Sharjah needs a thirty second announcement for its opening weekend. Write it for the ear, short sentences, one idea each. Pick a licensed stock voice. The first version mispronounces the neighbourhood name, Al Nahda, so you add a pronunciation hint and regenerate that line only. Then listen on a phone speaker at normal speed. It sounds clear and friendly. Total time, fifteen minutes.
```

Scene 7 — Voice-over tips

```text
Some voice over tips. Write for the ear, with short sentences and one idea each, and spell out numbers that could be misread. Add pronunciation guidance for names and acronyms. Use punctuation and line breaks to control pacing. And always listen on a phone speaker, because that is how most people will hear it.
```

Scene 8 — Consent, likeness, law

```text
The rules. Never clone a voice or create an avatar of a real person without explicit, informed and documented consent, and tools like ElevenLabs require verification for professional cloning. Never impersonate public figures or imply endorsements they have not given. Label realistic synthetic media as platforms require, and note that the EU AI Act's transparency obligations apply from August twenty twenty six. And get written agreements with voice actors and presenters covering AI use, duration, territories and how consent can be withdrawn.
```

Scene 9 — Voice-clone scams

```text
The same technology powers fraud. Imagine a finance assistant receiving an urgent voice note that sounds exactly like the chief executive, asking for a payment today. Defend against this with call back verification through known numbers for any payment or credential request, code words or two person approval for payments above a threshold, and training staff with real examples. A process beats a good ear every time.
```

Scene 10 — Business example: Dubai hospitality training

```text
A realistic example. A hospitality group in Dubai turns a written training manual into twelve short videos. The training lead approves every script, a licensed stock avatar presents in English, and dubbed Arabic versions are reviewed by two native speaking managers, who correct two terms for local usage. Captions are added in both languages, and each description notes that the presenter is AI generated. Production is far faster than filming, and updating a policy now takes hours, not a reshoot. When a policy changed three months later, updating the videos took an afternoon. The training lead edited two scripts, regenerated those sections in English, and the managers reviewed the regenerated Arabic lines. Under the old approach, the same change would have meant booking a presenter, a crew and a studio, so updates were simply not made.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes

```text
Four common mistakes. Cloning a voice just to test, without consent. Skipping native speaker review, which is how embarrassing mistranslations reach customers. Publishing realistic synthetic presenters without disclosure. And trusting urgent voice requests for payments. Every one of these has caused real harm to real organisations.
```

Scene 12 — Accessibility bonus

```text
One more benefit worth planning for. AI voice and captioning make your content accessible to people with hearing or visual impairments and to non native speakers, and captions help videos perform when people watch without sound. Always publish captions or transcripts, and check automatic captions carefully for names and technical terms, because that is where they usually go wrong.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me produce the Sharjah shop announcement. I paste the approved script, written for the ear, short sentences, one idea each. From the voice library I choose a licensed stock voice that is warm and clear. The first render mispronounces Al Nahda, so I add a pronunciation hint and regenerate only that paragraph, which saves time and credits. I also add a short pause before the opening hours, so they are easier to catch. Then I listen on a phone speaker at normal speed. It sounds friendly and clear.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Next, the Arabic version for the Dubai training videos. I generate the Arabic dub. Two native speaking managers review it and mark two terms. One is technically correct but sounds formal and foreign to local staff, the other is a hotel term used differently in the Gulf. I correct both lines and regenerate them. Then captions in Arabic and English, checked for names. In each video description I add a line saying the presenter and voice are AI generated. Consent records and the licence for the stock avatar are saved in the project folder.
```

Scene 15 — Recap and try this now

```text
Recap. Start with an approved script, use licensed or consented voices and avatars, generate in sections, get native review, add captions, and disclose. Never clone or impersonate without consent, and protect payments with call back verification. Try this now. Write a sixty second script using the voice over tips, generate it with a licensed stock voice if you have access, and check pacing and pronunciation on a phone speaker.
```

</details>

| 6 | 2:27 | 33 | Score each output from one to five on four things. Accuracy, meaning facts, numbers and citations. Instruction following, meaning every constraint met. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Comparing outputs side by side

```text
Every few weeks a new model arrives with claims that it is the best ever. How do you know whether it is better for your work? In this lecture you will learn to build a reusable test set from your real tasks, score outputs with a clear rubric, use AI to help judge without being fooled, and read results so you make good decisions, not just exciting ones.
```

Scene 2 — Why a test set

```text
Why build a test set? Because impressions are unreliable. The tool you used most recently, or the one with the prettiest interface, tends to feel better. And models change often, so a comparison from last spring may be wrong today. A fixed set of real tasks with success criteria lets you compare fairly, and re run the comparison in under an hour whenever something changes. AI engineers call this an evaluation set. You can build one without writing any code.
```

Scene 3 — The analogy

```text
Think of it like a driving test. Every candidate drives the same route, under the same rules, and passes or fails on clear criteria, not on how charming they are. Your test set is the route. Your rubric is the examiner's checklist. And every AI tool takes exactly the same test.
```

Scene 4 — Building the test set

```text
Build eight to fifteen tasks from your real work. A writing task, like a client update from bullet notes. A summary of a ten page report. An analysis of a campaign CSV. A research question needing a primary source. An instruction following task with a strict format, word limit and banned words. An honesty test, a question the material cannot answer. A language task if you work in Arabic, Urdu or another language, reviewed by a native speaker. And one task unique to your job.
```

Scene 5 — Criteria first

```text
Here is the key idea. Write the success criteria before you run anything. All key numbers present and correct. Every constraint met. Says not in the material when appropriate. If you write criteria after seeing the outputs, an impressive looking answer will quietly move the goalposts, and you will reward style over substance.
```

Scene 6 — The rubric

```text
Score each output from one to five on four things. Accuracy, meaning facts, numbers and citations. Instruction following, meaning every constraint met. Usefulness, meaning could you use it with light edits. And honesty, meaning it admits uncertainty and invents nothing. Add effort to fix in minutes, which often decides real value. And score blind where you can. Paste outputs as A, B and C, and ask a colleague to score without knowing which tool wrote which.
```

Scene 7 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. Two tools summarise the same ten page report in five bullets. B's summary is longer and reads beautifully. A's is plainer. You compare both against your reference facts from the report. A has every number right. B has one revenue figure wrong. A wins, clearly, despite being less impressive at first glance. That is exactly the kind of difference a rubric catches and a quick impression misses.
```

Scene 8 — AI as judge, carefully

```text
You can use an assistant to help score large test sets, but know its biases. AI judges tend to prefer longer answers, their own style or vendor, and confident tone. So give the judge your rubric and the reference facts, ask it to quote the evidence for each score, randomise the order and hide the tool names, and spot check a sample yourself. The AI helps with the volume. You make the final accuracy calls.
```

Scene 9 — Reading results

```text
Now read the results wisely. Look at failure modes, not just averages. A tool that averages four point two but occasionally invents financial figures may be unacceptable for finance work. Ignore tiny differences, like four point one versus four point zero, which are within normal variation. Weigh effort to fix and integration alongside the scores. And re test after major model releases.
```

Scene 10 — Business example: Lahore agency, 20 people

```text
A realistic business example. A twenty person agency in Lahore builds a twelve task test set from real client work, including an Urdu task and an honesty test, and runs it on three assistants. Two account managers score blind, an AI judge scores with quoted evidence, and a sample of its scores is checked by people. Two tools are close on average, but one invents a statistic in the research task. The agency standardises on the other, documents why, and schedules a re test in six months. Six months later, the agency reran the same twelve tasks when both vendors released new models. The tool they had chosen still led on the research task, but the other had fixed its invented statistics problem. Because the test set and past results were saved, they could see the change clearly and decided to keep their choice while adding the other tool for a specific drafting task.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes

```text
Four common mistakes. Test sets made only of easy tasks, which every tool passes. Criteria written after seeing the outputs. Trusting an AI judge without spot checks. And deciding on averages while ignoring a dangerous failure. Avoid those, and your comparison will be better than most published reviews.
```

Scene 12 — Keep the test set alive

```text
Treat your test set like a living document. Store the tasks, inputs, success criteria and reference facts in a shared folder with a version number. When your work changes, add tasks. When one becomes irrelevant, retire it. And keep past results, so when a vendor announces a new model, you can see whether it actually improved your scores, rather than relying on memory or marketing.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me build the Lahore agency's test set. In a sheet I list ten tasks from real client work, with columns for the input, the success criteria and the reference facts. Task six is an honesty test, a question the attached report cannot answer. Task nine is an Urdu message, to be reviewed by a native speaker. I fill every success criteria cell before running anything. Then I run all ten in two assistants, with identical prompts and files, and paste the outputs into a document as A and B, with tool names hidden.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
I paste the rubric, the reference facts and the outputs into the judge prompt, asking for quoted evidence and telling it not to reward length. The judge scores each row with quotes. I spot check three rows myself. In one, the judge rewarded B's longer answer despite a missed constraint, so I lower that score. Then I look for failure modes. In task four, B invented a market statistic. That single failure matters more than the averages. My summary recommends A for research and client facts, and says both are fine for internal drafting.
```

Scene 15 — Recap and try this now

```text
Recap. Build a reusable test set of eight to fifteen real tasks, write criteria first, score with the four part rubric plus effort to fix, use AI judges carefully, and decide on failure modes as well as averages. Try this now. Write ten tasks from your real work with success criteria, run them on two tools, and write a one page summary with your recommendation and the most important failure modes you found.
```

</details>
